It has come to my attention that during the
recent visit of a Japanese Training Ship to this harbor,
one of the students exhibited to a friend of mine a map
drawn in fine detail of the water lines, locations and
08
distances between the cities a considerable distance up
the Navigable Chehalis River.
The streets and other
unusual detail were well defined and apparently drawn
to scale.
Thinking that this should be brought to your attention,
OCT 8 6 1931
I am,
Very respectfully yours,
QM. Lacaster

SECRETARY'S OFFICE
Departrient ot State
Sir:-
812.52J 87/44 10/28/30.
During 1926 or 1927 (quoting from memory as
records of this sort of thing are not properly preserved
by individuals) the writer was fortunate enough, through
some confidential sources in Lower California, to disclose
a plot or plan on the part of the Japanese, in the event of
war, to poison the entire water supply of the west coast of
the United States and Canada.
The information was sent to an associate in
New York who was a close personal friend of the Secretary of
Sate in Coolidge's cabinet (Kellogg) and by him turned over
to the Secretary. Shortly thereafter President Coolidge
announced that he would personally take charge of the foreign
situation. The water poisoning plot was only a portion of
the entire plans as then revealed. You may recall that
shortly thereafter a moratorium was declared in Japan.
It has occurred to the writer that under the
eristing conditions and the contingencies likely to arise,
the matter should again be called to the attention of the
State Department in order that prompt and adequate steps may
be taken to effectively guard and protect the sources of water
supply and prevent such overwhelming catastrophe in those
distriets.  This would have to be done by the governments
and not by state or individual activities.
Respectfully,
Lostah
FEB 1 9 1932
G. O. DeVAUGHN.
FILED

Sir:-

In view of our present relations with Japan I feel it my
ain
duty as a war veteran to report whatever may possibly lead to
something useful for our Department of State to know.
894.202
A few weeks ago I received an Esperanto letter from Hyman
Ledeen of Los Angeles, Calif. in which he informed me that a
Mr. Inoue, General Passenger Agent of the State Railroads of
Japan, would soon be in Houston, Texas and that I could use him
to entertain an Esperanto group the way he did in Los Angeles;
but that he does not want any PUBLICITY.
Day before yesterday Mr. M. INoUE called on me accompanied
by Mr. and Mrs. aname A. Susuki, another Jap who speaks English
(Inoue says he can read Engliah , but can not speak it). He
speaks Esperanto) and is the "K" Line Gulf Representative of
I spoke with
the Mawasaki Kisen Kaisha at Orange, Texas.
them for nearly two hours in Esperanto. Both Japs avoided
anything that would bring them before the public. All theyare
for was to meet individual Esperantists..
5
Mr. Inoue will reside with Eaname Susuki in Orange , Tezas
for about week, will then go to Wew York where he expects tobe
the latter part of this month and in March he expects to be in
Washington, D. C. At his request I gave him the following names
and addresses of Esperantists in Washington, D.C.
Miss Frances Fernald, Translator, Office of Education.
Mr. Allan Davis, Prinoipal High Sohool of Commeree.
Miss Amy OmLeavitt , Music Teacher, 706 1lth Str. nw.
Mr. Kaname Susaki has not his name in any Esperanto directory
He did not encourage my coming to Orange , Texas, to make mey
sperantists and organize a club.
Very respectfuly,
Abraham Rudy
Ueteran Spanish-ameriaan Was.

I have the honor to transmit herewith for your infor-
mation photostatic copies of what appear to be memoranda and a
map which were found by Mr. Herbert Wilcox, owner of the Wilcox
Cafeteria, 428 South Spring Street, Los Angeles, California,
and delivered to the Los Angeles office of the Bureau of Inves-
tigation, which seem to refer to an alleged plan to dynamite
6

Since my departure from Japan in July, I have
been wishing to write you. I came to Los Angeles
representing the City of Tokyo to extend the offi-
cial invitation for the International Olympiad in
1940 wh1ch we hope to have in Tokyo.
During the last two months after the Tenth Olym-
piad in Los Angeles, I have been visiting different
c1ties in your country, and came to New York on Oct.
15th. I am going to leave for Cambridge tonight
to visit my Alma Mater, and will be back here on
next Sunday, Oct. 30th. I expect to sail home
from San Franc1sco on November 17th.
As Your Excellency know, I am very much inte-
rested in the promotion of the friendly relations
between America and Japan, I deplore the hysterical
tendencies of our two peoples, and the misinterpre-
tations of the policies of the two governments. I
shall be very anxious to see you in Washington and
to get the frank expression of your views, so that
I shall be able to return to Japan with a clear un-
derstanding of America's policy toward Japan. I
shall be honored if I could meet Secretary Stimson
while in Washington.
As there has been so much rumoraconcerning the
movement of the American navy in Japan, I should
1932
like to meet Admiral Pratt before I return to Japan,
FILED
so that I shall be able to bring to my people the
friendly message from America. I shall be very much
obliged to you if you will kindly assist me to see
your leaders.- both in naval and military circles.
Just before my departure from  apan, I called on
Ambassador Grew in your Embassy, and asked him to cen-

Dear Bir:
A number of schools, organiaations, churches,
and service clubs have requested us in the past to
supply speakers on Japan and on the many phases of
Japanese life and problems. We have been glad to
fulfill this need whenever possible, as we consider
it our duty to render our services toward promoting
international good will and mutual understanding.
Not all of the organizations, however, which might
have been interested, were aware that speakers were
available through the Information Bureau of the
Japanese Chamber of Commerce of New York.
Consequently, we are taking this opportunity
to announce to a few of the groups which may have an
occasion to hold discussions that speakers may be
secured if the requests come early enough to arrange
the schedule. A few among the many possible topics
for them are the various aspects of Japanese problems -
industrial, social, economic and foreign relations of
Japan, Japan in Manchuria, and the Far Eastern problems.
If at any time in the future you should be
interested in securing a speaker on these or similar
topics, we shall be glad to be of any assistance to you.
Sincerely yours,
Whuyasidrnga
Yoshiyasu Kunazawa
Executive Secretary

894.2021
DIVISION QF
Japanese Espionage.
Depertment of State
Commander Flanigan and Lieutenant MoCollum were
invited to call at Mr. Hornbeck's office to discuss the
procedure which should be followed with regard to the
activities of Japanese officers and civilians in the
United States, particularly a naval officer, Lieutenant T.
Arima, towards the securing of naval and military informa-
tion.
Commander Flanigan outlined briefly the diffioulties
Confidential
which his Department has been having with Japanese in this
regard. He read a letter which his Department had received
today from the Sturtevant Fan Company, stating that a Japa-
nese, who had presented himself at the offices of the company
ostensibly with a view to being employed by the company, was
discovered ransacking the desks and papers. Commander Flani
believed that the center of the organization for the collection
of naval information is the office of the inspector of supplies
for the Japanese Navy, which is situated in New York. He said
that

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
that recently the offioer in charge applied to the Navy
Department, through the Japanese Embassy, for permission to
inspect a large number of factories manufacturing munitions
of various kinds; that he then prevailed upon the Amerioan
naval officer for the distriot of New York to forward an
application to a munitions company for permission to visit
a factory which he knew would not be accessible if permis-
sion had been requested through the Navy Department. In
view of these and other facts, Commander Flanigan was very
anxious that the activities of Arima be profitably employed
in seouring evidence to support his belief that the Japanese
naval inspector in New York has general charge and supervision
of espionage activities.
Commander Flanigan thought that after the necessary
evidence had been secured, proceedings should be taken against
Arima and that the Japanese Government should be asked to
withdraw the office of the inspector at New York.
Mr. Hornbeck expressed some doubt as to the wisdom of
this course. He believed that a successful prosecution of
Arima would not have the effeot of bringing the activities
complained of to an end; that the Japanese would regard Arima's
conviction as merely a casualty; and that they would proceed
as they have in the past. He believed therefore that the best
effects would be gained by informing the Japanese Ambassador
that evidenoe had been gathered sufficient to conviot one of
the Japanese operators; that sufficient evidence had been
gathered to inoriminate others; and that the Department of
Justioe

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
Justice would proceed to bring action unless the activities
complained of were immediately brought to an end.
This suggestion was agreeable to both Commander Flanigan
and Lieutenant McCollum. After some further disoussion, it
was agreed that, in order to secure a conviction against Arima
which would not be open to the possible charge that the con-
viction had been based largely on the present state of feel-
ing in this country toward Japan, an effort would be made to
have Arima place himself in such a position that there would
be no doubt as to his guilt. It was recalled that Arima has
arranged to meet certain of the former enlisted men with whom
he has established contacts during the Thanksgiving holidays.
Agents of the Department of Justice would be asked to put
themselves in a position to effect his arrest, if this should
be decided upon, as soon as they have satisfied themselves
that incriminating evidence can be found in Arima's possession.
It was agreed that Arima should be kept under continued
observation, and that after the necessary evidence had been
collected, it should be decided whether the ciroumstances were
favorable to warn the Japanese Ambassador and the Naval Attaché
against a continuation of attempts to secure naval and military
information.

Mr. Crowley telephoned the Department this morning
before my arrival and requested that an officer call him
up to disouss the reported detention of five Japanese at
Kearney, New Jersey.
I telephoned Mr. Crowley as soon as his request was
brought to my attention.
Mr. Crowley said that the five Japanese under deten-
tion were suspeoted of having photographed American war-
ships under construotion at Kearney, New Jersey, and that
they had been taken into oustody and detained by New
Jersey State authorities. So far as Mr. Crowley knew,
Federal agents had instituted no oharge against the Japa-
nese, as they did not believe that they had any "juris-
diotion." Mr. Crowley understood that the State authori-
JAN 19 193!
ties were of the opinion that they could take action.
FILED
against only one of the Japanese, one Karaji Takahashi,
and that if any action were taken it would be on the
charge

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.95)
- 2 -
charge of driving without a license. Mr. Crowley was of
the opinion that the others would be released.
Mr. Crowley said that he did not know of any Federal
statute on whioh proceedings oould be instituted by
Justioe against the Japanese, but if this Department had
any suggestion to make he would be glad to give it con-
sideration. I replied that I would roport the matter to
my superiors and that I would communicate with him again.
I added that the matter appeared to me to be of partiou-
lar interest to the Navy Department and asked whether
that Department had been appropriately informed.
Mr. Crowley said that Captain Collins, in charge of the
New York office of Naval Intelligonce, was fully in-
formed of developments and he assumed that Captain
Collins had reported direot to headquarters.
NOTE: I oallod up Captain Piokens of Naval Opera-
tions, and he stated that he had not heard of the case,
but that he would look into it and communicate with me
later.

January 17th, 1934.
Honorable George Dern, Secretary,
United States War Department,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
I am writing you after reading an article in column 4, page 9,
eetion L of "The New York Times," under dcte f January 17th. This
article refers to "Five Japanese Seized For T: king Photos of lulaski
Skyway near Kearny, N.J."
I want to inform you of sonething th.t has been going on for
several years which I have observed first and while perforring ay duties
as Pullman Conductor on trains all over the United States, and particular-
1y on New York Central Train #29. This train carries cars from New York
to Chicago via both the M.C. nd the N.Y.C. Railroads. One of the M.C.
routed cars is a Niagara Falls "Stopover."
The passengers of this car are priveleged to leave all their
belongings in their respective berths, und to use te car as their hotel
during the day of their visit to the Falls. These  riveleges limited of
course to passengers holding thirouh Chicae tic ets.
So long as I operatel this train I recall that there was
scarcely a trip that we did not haul 4 T punese en. They always came in
parties of 4, there seldom being any change in the size o? the group, which
usually consisted of one elderly man and three youn er ones, ll of very
high intelligence.
These groups were lways well sup lied with caneras of varying
size including movies. They elways were up at dayli  t and began taking
pictures from Rochester west. I became curious und gave them special
observation, and I an positive thet there are hundreds of photos in Japan
of every railroad bridge from Rochester to Detroit :nd Chicugo.
On several occasione when the car was chilled with & cold draft
when full steam was on, I have c ught there Japs taking ovies while pas-
sing through industrial sections and towns. The would sit in the lower
berth (2 of them) with the window o uned and the curtain drawn and buttoned.
One man ould brace the camera while t e other cranked. Theu h I ave
seen some that were operated by electric motor with a wire lead into nother
suit-case upon which the camera case sat. These electric machines were
absolutely noiseless. They have many uabroken fils of every factory and
every foot of trackage from the east end of Buffalo to "iagera F lls .Y.
They were also well supplied wi th cameras that fit on the chest, arm nd
leg. And all seened to be of very expensive material and design.

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.98)
(COPY)
I tried to criticize them for some things that bothered
ors, they professed not to understand nor to be able to
Talls restaurant in language that I could not improve upon mysèlf.
I have seen pictures of power houses in C nada taken. from the
Tork side with the aid of telescopes attached in front of the camera.
nover failed to get a shot of the New York Central Station at Buffalo,
 Peace Bride, the Black Rock yards and railroed bridge, The Falls,
apids, the entire Niagara River, A erican Tower Touses from the land
ide and Cenadian Power Houses from the New York side.
I have seen some cameras equipped with compasses nd combination
watch and compass and separate compasses in their possession.
These observations were ade from 1928 to 1932 after wlhich
poriod I was furloughed and am still unemployed. I have mentioned this
fact to other conductors and on one or two occasions to some of the minor
office employees, but no one seemed to see anything alarming in it so I
never did make a written report of it until this letter to you.
In my travels over other parts of the country I have never seen
a Jap fail to photograph any bridge of any size over which we passed in
daylight hours, or any industrial center, nd written notations arc made
too of overy picture taken.
I am no alarrist, but in view of the fact that Japan will not
permit any pictures to be ta en by foreigners in her possessions, the
fact that they are continuously photogranhing our power sources and
industrial and railroad centers in such n apparently scientific systematic
menner added to the fact that the past adainistration did not seem to
know what it was all about, I an merely offerin: this infor ation w thout
comment for I realize that my opinion on an war problem woula not be
worth the pa er it was written on. But the above are facts, the value
of which can be properly appraised b: none other tn ycur own department.
Assuring you of ry belief that the entire population is
ploased with your appoint ent and is solidl behind the Tresident and
all administration moves: I am
Y. ure ost respectfully,

I can not, of course, know that the information which is to follow will

The Karges Furniture Co. of t.is city is one of the community's outstanding
organizations and the members of the Karges family are so outstanding that
their integrity can not be questioned. For some six or seven years they
had in their employ a Japanese artist iy te nume of Ryo Ohara. He is a man
of most remarkable skill and has been one of their valued employees. he has
always maintained such a nigh standard of honor and integrity that they have
looked upon him as a very loyal source of support in time of difficulty with
their workers. Not once tliroughout the period of time ne has been with them
have. they been able to lose sight of the fact tit he is no ordinary artist
employed to decorate fine furniture but a keen minced, intelligent person who
has no more business earming hi: living as a furniture decorator than I would 
have.
My wife's brother, Lr. Walter Judd, now at tue Mayo Clinic, Rochester, Minnesota
taking a three year special course with then, returned. from the interior of
China, Sha Vu province, when internal dissension reached a point that our
Government considered it wise to recall our medical missioneries. Through
him and nis years of contact with the Oriental mind and is having had personal
touch with the Chino-Japanese situation, I learned many things that I could
not have known otherwise.
Months ago, r. Edwin Karges allowed me to hire Mr. Ohara, his Japanese
decorator, to paint a picture to hang over a mantel in my bed room and to
decorate two screens to be placed before fireplaces. I talked with him at
length and we grew very friendly. Thereafter, because he was suc. a remarkable
person, I invited him to come to my home in the evening, curing whicn time
we discussed many things pertaning to the present t nse situation in the Far
East. There was no question in my mind but that he was pumping me and his
greatest interest seemed to be the attitude of the average American citizen
to ard Japan and tne Manchurian situation. I have nao him in my home six or
eight evenings, during which time we chattec, sometimes until midnight

activities of Japan where the League of Nations was concerned, fortifications
in Far Bastern waters, exactly what strategic points were being fortified
by France and England at this time, what Japan would be forced to do to meet
such emergencies, all were discussed with an understanding and glibness that
belied his humble position in Evansville. "hy he should be in this rather

I was interested in obtaining from Japan a special type of abrasive stone

"Mr. Wu leaves for New York tomorrow."

WILBUR BY THE SEA FLORIDA
APRIL 30 1934
INTELLIGENCE BUREAU
WASHINGTON D C
DEAR SIR:
On Saturday April 28th, a Japanese was admitted into this country at Key
West, Florida. On the trip from Key West to Miami a Mexican,
another American and I were seated together in the smoking compartment
of the day coach. The Jap came in and started conversation. He was drunk
and said more than he shou d have. From his conversation we gathered he was
on a toure He said he had a pass that would take him through any country-
He does not have a business yet he says he gets paid and doesnt drink while
on duty. He was headed for Washington, D. C. and said he was going to tour
the U. S. and go back to Japan soon. Said U.S. would regret having
recognized Russia in two years time. Spoke of "our weakness to
organize because we were an aggregate of many nationalities." The
three of us listening in on his conversation all believed him to be
a spy-I feel it is my duty to tell you of tnis man. He is well educated-
speaks Spanish, Englisin and Japanese. He is short, less than 5 feet tall
and has very small hands with very short thumbs.  If there is anything
else I can do to assist you on this matter, kindly inform me.
Very truly yours,
Charles Fred Gritzner.

FAR EASTERN AFFAINS

tel
Japanese Propaganda.
Department of State
894.20211
That portion of the memorandum, entitled "Japanese
which is descriptive of the machinery of Japanese propa-
ganda, is virtually obsolete. Many of the organs of
894.202
propaganda therein mentioned have disappeared, largely
IAn 92 - 137
DIVNVISIO OF
ECVED
or the reason that those journals which depend upon
ficial subvention were discovered to be ineffective
a means of propagating Japanese opinion. The system
of distributing information, whether tendentious or
2
otherwise, has been vastly improved since l92l in point of
9
both effectiveness and subtilty.
The improvement dates from the employment in 1923 by
2
the Foreign Office of two Americans, Frederick Moore and
Dallas MoGrew, who are mentioned in the original memorandum.
They taught the Foreign Office the technique of news dis-
tribution, and when McGrew resigned in 1928 he stated to
FILEID
the present writer that he had done so because the Japanese.
had learned everything he knew.
1937
The principal source of propaganda with regard to
Japan's international relations is the Bureau of Intelligence
of the Foreign Office. This Bureau, when created during the
War,
F

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
War, was placed under the direction of an Ambassador on
the waiting list. It worked ponderously, largely because
the chief (Baron Ijuin) had had no experience in press
work and, like most Japanese of the old school, resented
the growing influence of the press and of public opinion.
After he was replaced by Marquis Komura in 1921, Moore,
who was then the Foreign Office press adviser, used his
influence to cause the bringing about of a radical change
in the relations between the Chief of the Bureau and press
correspondents. Komura, who was an affable and approach-
able person, established excellent relations with news-
paper representatives, both Japanese and foreign. His
successors uniformly have been expansive and talkative
individuals, such as Saito, Shiratori and Amau, in contrast
with the dour and bureaucratic Ijuin; and, consequently,
the relations between the so-called "Foreign Office Spokes-
man" and press representatives are more informal, and
hence more satisfactory than they once were.
It may be added parenthetically that, in addition to
the daily conferences which the Chief of the Bureau of
Intelligence holds with foreign and Japanese representa-
tives, the Bureau employs a number of persons, such as
historians, international lawyers, and publicists, as well
as Japanese officials, in preparing studies of current
questions for distribution to editorial writers.
In

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.97)
-8 -
In 1925, the War Office set up a press office under
the direction of Colonel Sakurai, author of "Human Bullets",
a highly emotional account of the siege of Port Arthur,
and the Navy Department followed a year or two later with
its own office. These two offices were intended primarily
to reach the Japanese public, but since the Mukden Incident
they have sought more and more to establish contact with
foreign correspondents.
It is necessary at this point to note the distinction
between the Japanese system of news distribution and of
propaganda. At one time they were indistinguishable from
each other. In 1921, the Foreign Office had Yen 3,500,000
at its disposal for propaganda purposes, and a substantial,
if not greater, part of this fund was used to support
journals in various parts of the world; whereas today the
fund has shrunk to something over one million Yen. Many
of the papers which were supported by the Foreign Office
have gone out of existence; and the news agencies that
were once dependent on official subvention are, if not
paying ventures, at least on a sounder financial basis
than they once were.
Kokusai, a news agency affiliated with Reuter's,
mentioned in the previous memorandum, was closed down in
X
1924 and was replaced by the Rengo News Agenoy. This agency
is organised along the line of the Associated Press and is
owned by the thirteen newspapers which have exclusive rights
 In 1936, Rengo vas mesged tirith lte Nippou Dempo
to
Henws lisfuey, aund dhe meden fkirdeeed, the

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.99)
to the publication of Rengo foreign despatehes. These
despatches, incidentally, include inooming despatches
from the Associated Press, Havas, and Reuter's, with
whom Rengo has special arrangements. When Kokusai went
out of existence, the management of Rengo stated that care
would be taken to avoid the errors of the previous
organization, whose manager, an American named James R.
Kennedy, engaged in a notorious controversy with
McClatchy over the question of Japanese immigration, went
to Paris as press adviser to the Japanese Peace Delegation,
and otherwise laid himself open to the charge of carrying
on Japanese propaganda. So far as one can observe, dis-
semination of tendentious information is better ordered
under Rengo than under Kokusai: Rengo has behind it the
financial resources of its member papers, its relations
with the Government are less palpable, and it has not
become embroiled in political issues.
The foregoing contrast suggests what is believed to be
the most striking change since 192l in propaganda methods --
the assumption by private interests and individuals of the
major portion of propaganda work and of the costs involved
in that work.
It was estimated by competent persons that in l920
about two million yen were spent by the Japanese Government
in subsidizing Chinese- and English-language newspapers
published in China, such as the Peking STANDARD. It is
probablo

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.98)
-5 -
probable that at the present time also a large part of
the Foreign Office fund is being used in China for the same
purpose, but as the fund itself is only one-fourth of the
amount it once was, the Japanese Government is probably not
spending for this purpose in China as much as it used to
spend. In fact, the clear intimations that have come to the
Department during the past two years from Europe and
Latin-America of the lavish use of money to influence the
publication by local papers of information favorable to
Japan suggest the possibility that China is not, for the
moment, the prinoipal field for the expenditure of official
propaganda funds.
It is not practicable to enumerate all the channels
for the disbursement of private Japanese funds; but an
examination of periodicals published throughout the East
shows that advertising by important Japanese firms is
apparently one of the chief devices used to reward the
adoption by the periodicals of an attitude friendly to
Japan. A critical examination of periodicals favored by
Japanese advertisers will in many instances reveal an
illuminating discrepancy between the unimportanoe of the
periodicals as advertising media and the sums probably
spent for Japanese advertisements appearing therein.
It is becoming increasingly the practice of Japanese
of large means to defray the expenses of sending Japanese
publicists abroad. Jiuji Kasai, for example, who visited
the

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
the United States a year or two ago and delivered a number
of lectures in defense of Japan, and who had previously
made several visits to the United States, was a protegé
of Viscount Shibusawa. Mention also might be made of the
practice of bringing to Japan foreign correspondents on
sight-seeing jaunts, the cost of which is also largely
defrayed by private interests.
In conclusion, it may be observed that Japanese
propaganda is now conduoted through processes which are
not so easily discernible as they once were, for the reason
that private individuals and quasi-official organizations
are taking on an increasingly large part in the work of
disseminating propaganda material, even though the character
of such material is still laid down by the Japanese Govern-
ment.

former is one of the best known oriental
antique shops in the world, with branches
in London and Paris as well as in New York.

to be patronized by Mr. Stimson!

Arrest and Detention of Yoshio Matsuda,
Japanese Naval Inspector, at St. Petersburg,
Florida, on January 1, 1935.
Mr. Wixon, Department of Labor, says
he has talked with the immigration officers
at Tampa, Florida, who stated that Yoshio
Matsuda, arrested at St. etersburg, Florida,
January 1, has not been held in custody by
them at any time, and is now at large.
894.20211/140
Apparently he was arrested by the pclice
as a suspicious character and held for
questioning by the immigration officers,
a proceeding which required about two hours.
Department of Labor has telegraphed
the Commissioner of Immigration at Seattle
for a report of entry of the Jananese in
question, and the immigration authorities
at Tampa have been instructed to take no
action, other than that of a routine
nature, except on instruction from
Washington. (See Tokyo Visa Report,
October 13, 1933, attached, listing Yoshio
Matsuda as naval insrector, with rank of
Lieutenant Commander.)

January 2, 1935
SKH:
There is attached a press release in
regard to the arrest, etc., of a Japanese
naval officer in Florida, sent to me by
Colonel MacCormack of the Department of
Labor.
Colonel MacCormack informed me over the
telephone, in a second conversation, that
the arrest of a Japanese naval officer at
St. Petersburg, Florida, apparently came
about through information given to the
St. Petersburg police officials by a Depart-
ment of Justice agent on duty in or about
Jacksonville, Florida. He stated that this
fact would not be given to the press or
otherwise made public. Colonel MacCormack
spoke in commendatory terms of the immigra-
tion officer who secured the release of
the Japanese naval officer from police
custody, indicating that the delicate nature
of the situation had been realized by that
officer and acted upon with uncommon good
judgment. He further indicated that the
Department of Labor does not anticipate
the need for any further action on its part
in connection with this incident.
In talking with Colonel MacCormack
and Captain Puleston, Director of Naval
Intelligence, I have given to them such
information

CAMERAMAN NON GRATA!
YOSHIO MATSUDA
LIEUTENANT COMMANDER of the Japanese navy
allegedly took pictures of southern harbors and an Ameri-
can cruiser, and was forthwith confined in St. Peters-
burg jail. Later he was released without prosecution,
but is expected to make an explanation to his embassy
here. Photo by International.

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
AN29 19.15
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DAISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 8, 1935.
The attached memoranda are being held
in connection with the arrest of Lieutenant
Matsuda, a Japanese naval inspector, in
Florida. Among them is a memorandum of a con-
versation between the Japanese Counselor and
Mr. Turner. A reply to Mr. Fujii has been
promised, but as the information on hand
is inadequate for this purpose, I have asked
Mr. Wixon, Deputy Commissioner of Immigration
and Naturalization, for a comprehensive state-
mont of the circumstances involved in the
arrest of the Japanese. Mr. Wixon promised
this statement, and said that he would call
here shortly in regard to the matter. I
talked with him on the subject on January 7.
I spoke to Mr. Ward (Le) as to what
the character of the Tepartment's reply to
the Japanese Embassy should be. He strongly
felt that while Lieutenant Matsuda may
not have violated any law, nevertheless
his actions were such as to arouse suspicion,
and possibly to warrant the detention and
questioning which took place. He also
suggested that it seemed clear that Lieutenant
Matsuda was insufficiently identified, carry-
ing no passport, and that as soon as his
official status was definitely known he
was released. Lieutenant Matsuda, it
appeared to him, also was treated with
unusual

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
unusual consideration -- an immigration officer
going to the trouble to take him to a
theater, and to arrange for him to sleep
at his own home.
In view of these circumstances, Mr.
Ward feels that the Department of Labor,
in its explanation, should make out as
good a case as possible in justification
of the action of immigration authorities
in this case. He further believes that
this might be an opportune time to suggest
to the Japanese Embassy the possibility of
discouraging the taking of pictures by
Japanese Government officers in this
country, in circumstances likely to arouse
suspicion.
LDS/DLY

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.97)
E
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 4, 1934,
CONVERSATION:
FE
AFFAIRS
Mr. K. Fujii,
Counselor, Japanese Embassy.
Mr. Turner.
Departinont of State
Subject:
Arrest and Detention of Yoshio Matsuda
Japanese Naval Inspector, at St. Petersburg,
Florida, on January 1, 1935.
Mr. Fujii of the Japanese Embassy called at 4:30 p.m.
His call was in regard to the arrest at St. Petersburg,
894.20211/144
Florida, of Lieutenant Commander Yoshio Matsuda. He stated
that Lieutenant Commander Matsuda was, like many Japanese,
interested in photography and had been taking pictures of
Florida harbors, secure in the belief that no question of
violation of strategic zone laws was involved. He was
questioned by a policeman and escorted to a police station
where he was examined and then released after informing the
police officials that he was a Japanese naval officer.
On the following morning an immigration officer called
FEB.4-1935
at his hotel and he was forced to accompany the officer to
FILED
a jail where he was locked behind bars despite his protesta-
tions that he was a Japanese naval officer. I asked if
Lieutenant Commander Matsuda was struck or in any way
physically mistreated. Mr. Fujii replied that no physical
violence was involved, except that Lieutenant Commander
Matsuda was forcibly arrested and detained. Mr. Fujii stated
that

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
that Lieutenant Commander Matsuda had no passport with
him and that upon his arrest he requested that he be
permitted to communicate with the Japanese Consul or the
Embassy for purposes of identification. This request was
refused. He was detained behind bars for about four or
five hours and photographs were taken of him in that
situation which appeared in newspapers throughout the
country.
Mr. Fujii stated that he had been instructed by
his Ambassador to inquire into the matter and to request
that an explanation be obtained from the immigration
officials concerned. The Embassy desired to know why a
Japanese naval officer had been detained behind bars and
why he had not been permitted to communicate with the
Embassy. Mr. Fujii remarked that although American citizens
had been arrested on similar suspicion in Japan there had
been no cases of arrest and detention of American officials.
I interrupted to remark that I knew personally of cases of
arrest and detention of American officials, including
members of our Embassy staff, by Japanese officials.
The photographing of Lieutenant Commander Matsuda while
in jail seemed to be a particularly sore point. Mr. Fujii
stated that a Japanese officer would regard such treatment
as a deep disgrace and that the incident had caused much
excitement in Japan. He thought that such an incident was
particularly unfortunate in view of the general situation at
the

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
the present time. He considered it deplorable that a
naval officer of Japan travelling in this country should
be subjected to such an indignity. He stated that he
did not desire to make a protest, formal or informal,
but wished to obtain an explanation of the incident as
soon as possible.
I stated that I was not in a position to make a
reply at present but I would submit the matter to the
proper authorities and make a reply as soon as possible.
WTT/DLY

In 1929(?) during the visit of Anerican warships at

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
AN 23 1935
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January
Memorandum Regarding the Case of
ZCA
Lioutenant Commander Matsuda.
28 198
(Based on information received from the Navy
and Labor Departments)
Lieutenant Commander Yoshio Matsuda was apprehended
between 10:00 and 11:00 a.m., December 31, 1934, on the
water front of St. Petersburg, Florida, on complaint of
the City Manager. He was taken to Police Headquarters
for questioning, and films from a moving pioture camera
894.20211
and another camera were taken up by the police with the
promise that they would be returned if nothing objeotion-
able were found. On the understanding that he would re-
turn on the following morning, he was released.

the laws of this country are exceedingly lenient as regards
the matter of espionage, in recent months Japanese
officials, who are visiting this country in increasing
numbers, have made themselves conspicuous on a number
of occasions by actions that can not but be interpreted
as extremely suspicious.

CONCERNING ACTIVITIES OF LT. COMMANDER YOSHIO MATSUDA. CONSTRUCTION CORPS.
IMPERIAL JAPANESE NAVY. ATTACHED TO THE JAPANESE NAVAL INSPECTION OFFICE.
One Madison Avenue. New York City. IN ST. PETERSBURG. FLORIDA. ON 31 DECEM-
BER.1934.
On Wednesday, 2 January, 1935, Captain Tamon Yamaguchi, Imperial
Japanese Navy, Japanese Naval Attaché, came without notice to the reception
room of the Director of Naval Intelligence and requested immediate permis-
sion to see him. The Director was engaged at the time and had to keep Cap-
tain Yamaguchi waiting a few minutes. He sent in word ? second time that
he would like very much to see the Director as soon as possible.
The Director then admitted Cantain Yamaguchi, who came in plainly
very much agitated over a long listance telephone conversation he had held
the night before with a Japanese Naval officer, Lieutenant Commander Yoshio
Matsuda, Construction Corps, Imperial Japanese Navy, attached to the Japanese
Naval Inspection Office, 1 Madison Avenue, New York City.
Captain Yamaguchi stated that during the orevious night Matsuda
had called him up from St. Petersburg, Florida, and stated that he had been
arrested for taking pictures of the Cruiser TRENTON and the waterfront of
St. Petersburg. Captain Yamaguchi stated that he very much regretted this
action on the part of Lt. Commander Matsuda and requested that he, Captain
Yamaguchi, be permitted to handle this matter infornally. He said that he
was prepared to send Lt. Co mander Matsuda home. He cited the case of the
Assistant Naval Attaché, Lieutenant Kanamoto, who had been arrested in San
Diego in January, 1934, and returned to Japan, and said that he had warned
his officers to avoid similar activities. The Director of Naval Intelligence
gathered the impression that Captain Yamaguchi was sincerely concerned about
the matter, and really regrette that a Japanese Naval officer should be ap-
prehended in such conduct, and that he was desirious of closing the incident
as quietly and expeditiously as possible. However, as the Director was not
informed of the circumstances, he only told Captain Yamaguchi that he would
have to wait developments.
At that time, Captain Yanaguchi was better informed of what Lt. Com-
mander Matsuda had actually done than was the Director of Naval Intelligence,
for Captain Yamaguchi had been in communication over the telephone with Matsuda
and had been thoroughly informed by Matsuda of his activities. That is to say,
Captain Yamaguchi, knowing precisely what Matsuda had done, made a full apology
for his action, and his one concern was to handle the matter of his subordinate's
action informally and not through formal official channels.
The circumstances that caused the detention of Lt. Commander Matsuda
are briefly as follows: -
On Sunday afternoon, 31 December, he was observed by the Chief of De-
tectives, St. Petersburg, Florida, taking pictures from the Recreation Pier.
He was equipped with several cameras and was taken to headquarters for inves-

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
onfidential
-2
tigation. Among the objects that he had were pictures from the "pier, north,
south, and east - - - He motored in a taxi, taking pictures from the Spa. He
took pictures of the north and south yacht bases and a blimp hangar. He had
in his possession one Zeiss camera, very high grade, which takes 35 pictures
on one film. He also had a moving picture camera which takes 15 pictures. In
the Zeiss camera he had taken 17 pictures from different points along the wa-
terfront, including the Municipal Pier, the picture of the TRENTON and her po-
sition, also shots north and south of the TRENTON and shots south of the pier".
Additional information indicates that Matsuda had also gained compre-
hensive information regarding the ater supnly and waterfront activities of
St. Petersburg. He also admitted that enroute from New York to St. Petersburg
he had taken pictures at each stop inclu ing Richmond, Columbia, South Carolina,
Jacksonville and Miami. These ictures he forwarded to New York for transmittal
to his Government.
The Director of Naval Intelligence was informod by Colonel MacCormack,
of the Bureau of Imaigration, that on beir irst detained, Lt. Commander
Matsuda had no passport or pa ers to iniicate his stat s ind that he was de-
tained in order to establish his identity. It is hard to conceive that Lt.
Commander Matsuda did not know that he should cerry his identification papers
with him.
In addition, it is understood that he misrenresented his identity at

January 16, 1935.
On January 4, 1935, Mr. Fuji1, Counselor of the
Japanese Embassy, called at the Department of State and
made informal inquiries in regard to the reported deten-
tion on January 2, 1935, of Lieutenant Commander Yoshio
Matsuda, of the Japanese Navy, at St. Petersburg, Florida.
The appropriate authorities were immediately notified by
the Department of State of Mr. Fujii's inquiries, and they
were requested to cause an investigation to be made of the
circumstances of the incident under reference. Reports
8
have now been received from these authorities, and the
394.202
essential facts are believed to be as follows:
1. Lieutenant Commander Matsuda was apprehended on
the morning of December 31, 1934, on the water front at
1/1
St. Petersburg, Florida. He had in his possession a camera
with long-range lense and a moving picture camera. He was
48
taken to police headquarters for questioning. He had on
his person no passport or other papers of identification.
His cameras were taken up by the police with the promise
that they would be returned if nothing objectionable was
found, and, on the understanding that he would return on
E
B
the following morning, he was released.
4-1935
2. At no time was Lieutenant Commander Matsuda
formally arrested or confronted with police charges.
FE8 4- 1935
3. From about 11 a.m. on January 1 until about 10 a.m.
on January 2, he was under detention by immigration officials
who,

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
who, in the absence of his papers, were uncertain of his
status.
4. Between 4 p.m. and 8:45 p.m. on January 1, he
was detained in a police cell. During the remainder of
the period of his detention, he was in the personal
custody of an immigration official.
5. As soon as his official character was definitely
established he was released.
In the light of the above, the Department feels that
this unfortunate incident was primarily due to Lieutenant
Commander Matsuda's own inadvertence in having been engaged
in activities which laid him open to suspicion and in not
having had in his possession his papers of identification;
nevertheless, at the same time, the Department regrets that
Lieutenant Commander Matsuda was subjected to detention and
that photographing of him while under detention was not
prevented.
On the occasion of his call above-mentioned, Mr. Fujii
expressed desire that he be informed of the reasons for the
failure of the local authorities to afford Lieutenant
Commander Matsuda an opportunity to communicate with repre-
sentatives of his Government before he was placed under
detention. It has been ascertained that Lieutenant
Commander Matsuda communicated by telephone with the Japa-
nese Consulate General at New York and the Japanese Embassy
on the same day that he was placed under detention.
It

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
It has been noted that an inoreasing number of Japa-
nese officials sojourning in this country have made them-
selves conspicuous by engaging in activities extraneous to
their regularly assigned duties and of a character likely
to arouse suspicion. The reports received from the
authorities concerned indicate that certain actions of
Lieutenant Commander Matsuda, such as that of taking photo-
graphs of strategically important points, warrant the view
that he had conducted himself in a regrettable manner.
That this view is shared by the Naval Attaché of the
Japanese Embassy is indicated by the fact that the Naval
Attaché expressed to an officer of the Navy Department
regret that a Japanese naval officer should have been
apprehended in the circumstances which attended the case
of Lieutenant Commander Matsuda and offered spontaneously
to arrange for the termination of that officer's assignment
in the United States.

I sent for the Japanese Ambassador this morning and
read to him paragraphs 1 to 5 of the accompanying memorandum.
I then talked to him along the lines of the balance of the
memorandum and expressed regrets that Matsuda had been
detained and photographed behind bars. I said that this
message could have been very readily sent to him through
894.202
Mr. Fujii, but that I had really wanted the opportunity of
speaking to the Ambassador himself with regard to these
extraneous activities of Japanese naval officers in the
United States. I said that this was not the first case
that had arisen by any means; that as a matter of fact
we knew a good deal about their activities, although we
had said nothing about it. It seemed to me a very childish
6
and foolish procedure, yet when the press got hold of
cases a great deal was made of it. It certainly did not
E
tend to help good relations between the two countries

we were strongly against our own people doing anything
of a similar nature in Japan, and I felt sure that he

on his own initiative and traveling as a private citizen;
nevertheless, as soon as the matter came to the attention
of the Japanese authorities Matsuda had received a reprimand
from Tokyo. Furthermore, the head of the Japanese Inspection
Office in New York had suggested that Commander Matsuda
might well be relieved of his duties in that connection,
and that after discussing the matter [presumably with the
Embassy] the decision had been reached to let him remain
until May, at which time his term of duty would expire
anyway. Mr. Saito appeared to take my communication to
him very affably and conveyed the impression that he would
do what he could to stop the practice in the future.

JAPAWBSE PROPAGANDA IN AMERICAN PUBLIC SCHOOLS
Japan is adding to her elaborate and frank system of propaganda in the
United States a plan thru which pupils in the high schools throughout this country are
to use text books prepared in Japan and giving the Japanese point of view on matters
national and international.
Protest was made to the Territorial Sohool Commissioners of Hawaii by the
Sino-Korean Peoples League of Honolulu, in a letter, December 1l, 1934, against coun-
tenancing suoh a plan. In that letter it is stated that the plan was approved at a
Departmental Conferenoe of the Japanese Government at Tukyo, November 21, 1934; that
there were present thereat the Department heads for Foreign Office, Education Depart-
ment, Cultural Bureau, Overseas Education Institution, Promotion of International Cul-
tural Relations Society, and Prince Fushimi, Chief of the Japanese Naval Supreme War
Council, the founder of Hawaii's Prince Fushimi Scholarship Association, incorporated
in 1924.
Setsueehi Aoki, General Secretary of the Promotion of International Cul-
tural Society, said, "For several years the sohool heads of Hawaii and America with the
aid of many more influential educators and missionaries made a thorough survey of
Amerioan text books for the Japanese Society. The survey revealed that if proper prop-
aganda is systematically carried out in the sohools the Amerioan children will become
strong friends of Japan."
Conoerning the proposed free distribution of Japanese text books in our
publio sohools, Mr. Okada, the head of the Cultural Bureau and former Consul General
of Hawaii, stated "that all the necessary arrangements have been made with the Publio
Instruotion, also with the various religious and social leaders in Hawaii".
The plan includes also distribution among all the Japanese language schools
in Hawaii and Amerioa of "educational material concerning the vital national defense
poliey of Japan", and also the distribution free of oharge to the teachers of all pub-
lio sohools in Hawaii of "reading material, portfolios containing Japanese piotorials
and other graphic materials for school room use, etc."
The letter of the Sino-Korean Peoples League concludes as follows:
"Undoubtedly you are aware of the significance of such projects in Hawaii. We are very
deeply touohed and concerned with the aotion of the Publio Instruction taking a hand
aiding in spreading Japanese propaganda in Hawaii. We believe the result will be dis-
astrous to Americanization of the American citizens of Japanese ancestry. God knows
they have enough contact with the things of Japan and the Yamato and Bushido Spirit in
their homes and in the language schools. Why let the Japanese Government promote Jap-
anim even in the publio schools?"
Confirmation of these statements is found in a news item published Deoem-
ber 3, 1934, in the "Japan Times and Mail" of Tokyo, a newspaper maintained by the Jap-
anese Goverment for the purpose of presenting to readers of the English language the
Japanese Governmont point of view on current matters. The artiole is headed, "High
ahool Studonts in U. S. To Be Given Books With Correct Information on Japan", and
bates: "The Society of International Cultural Relations at its meeting Monday after-
oon deoided on  plen to be followed for the compilation of sohool text-books to be
sed for diffusing correct information on Japan. * * * * Material of instruotion will
Pover almost every phase of modern Japan, embracing topios on the government, economic
d soeial conditions, art, eto., whioh will be presented in a way that will appeal to
ho average high sehool student in America."
Japan's "International Cultural Bureau" is allowed 2,500,000 Yan in the
Office Budget for l935, while the recently created "Society for aternational
ral Relations" is supported by liberal contributions from Mitsui and Mitsubishi.

V.S. MOCLATCHY
JAPAN'S PSYCHOLOGY AND AMERICAN INTERESTS
The Japanese people believe, their leaders including the frank and forceful
Matsuoka, proolaim, and their school children are taught that Japan, as the only
netion whose ruler is Heaven-descended, is destined to rule the world. That belief
diotates the polioy and acts of Japan and of the Japanese.
That polioy received formal declaration at the ceremonial naming of the new
Heir to the Japanese throne when the Court Scholar, Dr. Ichimura, quoted from the
anoient olassics this language: "The essence of ruling the people lies in the enlight-
ening of them. * * * If there are persons not yet properly governed, it is because
they have not yet benefitted by the Imperial rule. * * * If those who have not yet re-
ceived enlightenment under the Imperial rule are found they are to be subjugated."
A knowledge of Japanese psychology in such matters makes understandable the
oalm ruthlessness with which Japan during the present century has defied protests and
ignored or violated agreements and treaties, and conquered and annexed adjacent terri-
tory and held it under commercial or military control in furtherance of her varied
interests; it will explain to some extent the air of placid superiority with which she
suggests that certain acts of other nations displease her sensitive people and should
cease--for instance the inexcusable act of the United States in floating its Navy on
the Pacifio Ocean, and our recently announced intention to construct the Nicaragua
Canal, which prompted Japan to prefer a request to Nicaragua that she be granted
equal rights in construction and control of such Canal; it will expiain why Japan
accepts compliance with demands of this character as recognition of the position to
which the Gods have assigned her and entertains corresponding contempt for the nations
which, as she assumes, thru weakness or fear, comply with the demands; it will explain
the calm assurance with which she openly propagandizes in this country for accomplish-
ment of her purposes and asks and secures the aid of American citizens and interests
in furtherance thereof.
It may even enable us to appreciate the humor in her latest move--a well de-
fined plan announced in the Japan Times of Tokyo, English language spokesman for
the Foreign Office, December 4, 1934, to introduce into our public school curriculum
a system of text books to be prepared in Japan with friendly counsel from a represen-
tative of the Institute of Pacific Relations, and designed to cover all matters on
which Japan wishes this country to be "properly informed".
In conneotion with that plan the General Secretary of the Society for Interna-
tional Cultural Relations reported that a thorough survey made for it by "the sohool
heads of Hawaii and America with the aid of many more influential educators and mis-
sionaries * * * revealed that if proper propaganda is systematically carried out in
the schools American children will become strong friends of Japan." *
The Sino-Korean Peoples League of Hawaii made, in December, a strong protest to
the Territorial Commissioners of Education against the introduotion of such text books,
deolaring that the result "will be disastrous to Americanization of the American cit-
izens of Japanese ancestry"; that "they have enough contact with the things of Japan

ROLOASed 2-13-35 JAPAWESE PROPAGANDA IN AMERICAN PUBLIC SCHOOLS
Japan is adding to her elaborate and frank system of propaganda in the
United States a plan thru which pupils in the high sohools throughout this country are
to use text books prepared in Japan and giving the Japanese point of view on matters
national and international.
Protest was made to the Territorial School Commissioners of Hawaii by the
Sino-Korean Peoples League of Honolulu, in a letter, December 1l, 1934, against coun-
tenanoing such a plan. In that letter it is stated that the plan was approved at a
Departmental Conference of the Japanese Government at Tukyo, November 21, 1934; that
there were present thereat the Department heads for Foreign Office, Education Depart-
ment, Cultural Bureau, Overseas Education Institution, Promotion of International Cul-
tural Relations Society, and Prince Fushimi, Chief of the Japanese Naval Supreme War
Counoil, the founder of Hawaii's Prince Fushimi Scholarship Association, incorporated
in 1924.
Setsueohi Aoki, General Secretary of the Promotion of International Cul-
tural Society, said, "For several years the sohool heads of Hawaii and America with the
aid of many more influential educators and missionaries made a thorough survey of
American text books for the Japanese Society. The survey revealed that if proper prop-
aganda is systematically carried out in the schools the Amerioan children will become
strong friends of Japan."
Concerning the proposed free distribution of Japanese text books in our
public schools, Mr. Okada, the head of the Cultural Bureau and former Consul General
of Hawaii, stated "that all the necessary arrangements have been made with the Publio
Instruction, also with the various religious and social leaders in Hawaii".
The plan includes also distribution among all the Japanese language schools
in Hawaii and Amerioa of "educational material concerning the vital national defense
policy of Japan", and also the distribution free of charge to the teachers of all pub-
lic sohools in Hawaii of "reading material, portfolios containing Japanese piotorials
and other graphio materials for schocl room use, etc."
The letter of the Sino-Korean Peoples League concludes as follows:
"Undoubtedly you are aware of the signi icance of such projects in Hawaii. We are very
deeply touched and concerned with the a :on of the Public Instruction taking a hand
aiding in spreading Japanese propaganda . Hawaii. We believe the result will be dis-
astrous to Americanization of the Americ.. citizens of Japanese ancestry. God knows
they have enough contact with the things
Japan and the Yamato and Bushido Spirit in
their homes and in the language schools.
y let the Japanese Government promote Jap-
anism even in the publio schools?"
Confirmation of these statements is found in a news item published Deaem
ber 3, 1934, in the "Japan Times and Mail" of Tokyo, a newspaper maintained by the Jap-
anese Government for the purpose of presenting to readers of the English language the
Japanese Government point of view on current matters. The article is headed, "High
Sohool Students in U. S. To Be Given Books With Correct Information on Japan", and
states: "The Sooiety of International Cultural Relations at its meeting Monday after-
noon decided on a plan to be followed for the compilation of school text-books to be
used for diffusing correct information on Japan. * * * * Material of instruction will
oover almost every phase of modern Japan, embracing topics on the government, economio
and social conditions, art, etc., which will be presonted in a way that will appeal to
the average high sohool student in America."
Japan's"International Cultural Bureau" is allowed 2,500.000 Yen in the
Foreign Office Budget for l935, while the recently created "Society for International
Cultural Relations" is supportod by liberal contributions from Mitsui and Mitsubishi.

JAPAN'S PSYCHOLOGY AND AMERICAN INTERESTS
The Japanese people believe, their leaders including the frank and forceful
Matsuoka, proolaim, and their school children are taught that Japan, as the only
nation whose ruler is Heaven-descended, is destined to rule the world. That belief
diotates the policy and acts of Japan and of the Japanese.
That polioy received formal declaration at the ceremonial naming of the new
Heir to the Japanese throne when the Court Scholar, Dr. Ichimura, quoted from the
anoient olassics this language: "The essence of ruling the people lies in the enlight-
ening of them. * * * If there are persons not yet properly governed, it is because
they have not yet benefitted by the Imperial rule, * * * If those who have not yet re-
ceived enlightenment under the Imperial rule are found they are to be subjugated."
A knowledge of Japanese psychology in such matters makes understandable the
calm ruthlessness with which Japan during the present century has defied protests and
ignored or violated agreements and treaties, and conquered and annexed adjacent terri-
tory and held it under commernial or military control in furtherance of her varied
interests; it will explain to some extent the air of placid superiority with which she
suggests that certain acts of other nations displease her sensitive people and should
cease--for instance the inexcusable act of the United States in floating its Navy on
the Paoific Ocean, and our recently announoed intention to construct the Nicaragua
Canal, which prompted Japan to prefer a request to Nicaragua that she be granted
equal rights in construction and control of such Canal; it will expiain why Japan
accepts compliance with demands of this character as recognition of the position to
whioh the Gods have assigned her and entertains corresponding contempt for the nations
whioh, as she assumes, thru weakness or fear, comply with the demands; it will explain
the calm assurance with which she openly propagandizes in this country for accomplish
ment of her purposes and asks and secures the aid of American citizens and interests
in furtheranoe thereof.
It may even enable us to appreciate the humor in her latest move--a well de-
fined plan announced in the Japan Times of Tokyo, English language spokesman for
the Foreign Office, December 4, 1934, to introduce into our public school curriculum
a system of text books to be prepared in Japan with friendly counsel from a represen-
tative of the Institute of Pacific Relations, and designed to cover all matters on
which Japan wishes this country to be "properly informed".
In connection with that plan the General Seoretary of the Society for Interna-
tional Cultural Relations reported that a thorough survey made for it by "the school
heads of Hawaii and America with the aid of many more influential educators and mis-
the sohools American children will become strong friends of Japan."
The Sino-Korean Peoples League of Hawaii made, in December, a strong protest to
the Territorial Commissioners of Education against the introduction of such text books,
declaring that the result "will be disastrous to Americanization of the American cit-
izens of Japanese ancestry"; that "they have enough contact with the things of Japan
in their homes and in the Japanese language sonooIs, and why promote Japanism in the
publio sohools.
Imungine the reaction in Japan if the United States announced preparation by it
of text books for use in Japan's public sohools to "properly inform" Japanese pupils
on subjeets of ooncern to us!
* C.J.I.C. Release #4O7, whioh will be mailed on request.

2-6-35

JAPAN PROPOSES TO INSTRUCT OUR PUBLIC SCHOOL STUDENTS
The Japan Times and Mail, English language voice of the Foreign Office of Japan,
in its issue of December 4th last, published an interesting item headed, "High School
Students In U.S. To Be Given Pooks With Correct Information on Japan". The article
states that the Society of International Cultural Pelntions has adopted a plan for com-
pilation of text books for the nurpose indicated, to be prepared in Japan by a special
committee with suggestions from u representative of the Institute of Pacific Relations.
The books will cover, it is stated, "aInnst every phase of modern Japan . . . in a way
that will appeal to the average high school student in America".
 The Society of International Cultural kelations is an organization supported by
such powerful commercial interests as Mitsui, Mitsubishi, etc. It acts under direction
of the Government Bureau of Cultural Relations, which has been allowed an appropriation
of 2,500,000 Yen in the 1935 bulget. "Cultivating cultural relations", is Japanese for
"inviting foreign approval of Janan's point of view". The Japanese delegates to the
Institute of Pacific Relations, at its recent bi-ennial sessions--and particularly at
that held at Banff, Canada, in l933, as shown by news reports--sought to secure the co-
operation of American delegates thereto to obtain from the United States an immigration
quota for Japan.
USE OF THE BOOKS PROTESTED IN HAWAII
Further information as to the text book plan is found in a letter of protest
addressed by the Sino-Korean Peoples League of Honolulu to the Territorial School Com-
missioners of Hawaii, December 1l, 1934. Therein it is declared that the plan was
approved at a meeting of departmental heads of the Japunese government, including the
Foreign Office, at Tokyo, Hovember 21, 1934. At that meeting, Mr. Okada, head of the
Bureau of Cultural Relations and former Consul General of Haweii, is quoted as saying,
concerning the proposed free distribution of Japanese text books, that "all the neces-
sary arrangements have been made with the Public Instruction and also with the various
religious and sociul leaders of Huwaii".
Setuechi Aoki, General Secretary of the Society of International Cultural Rela-
tions, is quoted as stating that "for several years the school heads of Hawaii and
America, with the aid of many more influcntial educators and missionaries, made a tho-
rough survey of Anerican text books for the Japanese society. The survey revealed that
if proper propaganda is systemetically carried out in the schools the American children
will become strong friends of Japan".
The plan includes also distribution emong all the Japanese language schools in
Hawaii and America of"oducational materinl concerning the vital national defense policy
of Japan".
The letter of the Sino-Koreun Peoples League protests against such proposed use
of the public sohools "in spreading Japanese propaganda in Hawaii" and declares that
"the result will be disastrous to Americanization of the American citizens of Japanese
ancestry".

Japanese with good-will of these films of Japan are now
to be shown for the first time.
The film is standard size and up to date in every

industries,no more complete and modern picture of present
day of Japan is available than these films which have been
specially prepared for the presentation to American audiences.
Titles and explanatory remarks are entirely in English.
I am fully equipped end furnish my own machine and
orerator,etc.,so there is no expense to you in this respect.

DIVISION OF
Dear Dr. Hornbeok:
Because of its influence on conditions in Hawaii and California,
and on relations between this nation and Japan, you will be interested in the Japan-
ose language sohool situation. Note in C.J.I.C. Doc. #425, herewith, a brief state-
mont thereof, additional details of which can be had if desired.
Investigation of these schools in California was made at my insti-
gation some 14 or 15 years ago. It was successful primarily because I was fortunate
onough to secure as translator Dr. B. C. Haworth, now and for some years past with
N.I. at Washington.
Under the U. S. Supreme Court decision neither California nor
Hawaii may exercise control over suoh schools. The Federal Government may not inter-
2
fere with the states in the matter of education. But it is asserted with good reason
0
that these schools as now conduoted constitute a serious menace to American citizen-
2
ship and to national welfare. If so, it would seem illogical to insist that there
does not reside in the Government the power to correct the evil.
Of late, leaders of the Japanese American Citizens League--a power-
1
ful coastwide organization of "Nisei", Americen born citizens of Japanese ancestry
whose avowed purpose is to prepare its memoers for their duties as loyal and efficient
Amerioan citizens, have realized an embarrassing position in which they are placed on 00
account of these schools. So long as the schools are maintained under present control
and polioy they will create a growing public distrust of the citizenship of those who
attond them, thus creating possible friction in California and misunderstanding with
Japan.
It has been suggested that if the study of the Japanese language
were added to the public high school curriculum the separate lan guage schools could
be abandoned with benefit to the Japanese themselves. The Joint Committee has main-
tainad friendly contact with the leaders of the Japanese American Citizens League in
the interest of better understanding (see C.J.I.C. Doo #421 and #417 enclosed) and
has discussed this subjeet with them. I have mentioned the matter to the State Sup-
erintendent of Public Instruction and at his invitation will take it up with him
after my return to office duties next month.
I give you this pioture believing that you will find therein evi-
dence that our committee is working--perhaps with seeming brusqueness at times--in
proteotion of national interest and to remove present and prevent future friction
with our neighbors across the Pacific. Any future trouble with Japan, in my judgment,
will not be oreated by what was done by Congress in l924. It may be occasioned by
misunderstanding or misrepresentation thereof and by constant prodding on the part
of Japan and her American allies. The services of this committee--unacknowledged of
course, and repudiated if advisable--may have a value. We can be brutally frank 
and when necessary. The State Department may not.
If time permits I will write you later as to the case of the text

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.94)
beok on Japan prepared for use in public schools of Hawaii and the States. Apparenty
we have won there.
Believe me with personal regards and appreoiation of numerous
courtesies,
Sincerely yours,
.. cialby
V. S. McCletchy
VSM.DK

[PUBLIO—NO. 162—74T CONGRESS]
[8. 2508]
AN ACT
To authorize the naturalization of certain resident alien World War veterans.
Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the
United States of America in Congress assembled, That notwith-
standing the racial limitations contained within section 2169 of the
Revised Statutes of the United States, as amended (U. S. C., title
8, sec. 359), and within section 14 of the Act of May 6, 1882, as
amended (U. S. C., title 8, sec. 363), any alien veteran of the World
War heretofore ineligible to citizenship because not a free white
person or of African nativity or of African descent may be natural-
ized under this Act if he
(a) Entered the service of the armed forces of the United States
prior to November 11, 1918;
(b) Actually rendered service with the armed forces of the
United States between April 6, 1917, and November 11, 1918;
(c) Received an honorable discharge from such service for any
reason other than his alienage;
(d) Resumed his previous permanent residence in the United
States or any Territory thereof; and
(e) Has maintained a permanent residence continuously since
the date of discharge and is now a permanent resident of the United
States or any Territory thereof; upon compliance with all the
requirements of the naturalization laws, except—
(f) No certificate of arrival and no declaration of intention shall
be required;
(g) No additional residence shall be required before the filing
of petition for certificate of citizenship; and
(h) The petition for certificate of citizenship shall be filed with a
court having naturalization jurisdiction prior to January 1, 1937.
Src. 2. Certificates of citizenship heretofore issued and heretofore
granted by any court having naturalization jurisdiction under the
provisions of the Act of May 9, 1918, or of the Act of July 19, 1919,
to any alien veteran who is eligible to be naturalized under the
provisions of section 1 of this Act, and orders or judgments
authorizing such certificates, are hereby declared to be valid for all
purposes insofar as the race of the veteran is concerned. Such
certificates may be stamped, declaring their validity under this Act,
by the Commissioner of Immigration and Naturalization upon sub-
mission of satisfactory proof to establish identity.
Certificates declared valid under the foregoing paragraph, which
have been lost, mutilated, destroyed, or surrendered to any official
of the United States may be replaced by a new certificate bearin
date of original certificate upon compliance with the provisions of
section 32 (a) of the Act of June 29, 1906, as amended.
Sec. 3. On applications filed for any benefits under this Act, the
requirement of fees for naturalization documents is hereby waived.
Approved, June 24, 1935.

CONGRESSIONAL RECOGNITION O JAPANESE VETERANS
It seems probable that the bill H. R. 7170, introduced by Hon. Clarence F. Lea,
anese veterans who accepted service under the oolors during the World War, though en-
titled to exemption as aliens, will be passed by Congress. It has already been recom-
mended by the House Immigration Committee without dissenting vote.
At the close of the Great War a Congressional act granted the privilege of imme-
diate naturalization to aliens who had served with the colors. Several hundred Japan-
ese were made citizens under that law by the U. S. Federal Court in Hawaii. Subse-
quently that action was set eside by the U. S. Supreme Court, which declared that the
law intended only to shorten the probationary period for aliens eligible to natural-
ization and not to confer the right of naturalization on those ineligible therete.
Last summer, while the American Legion and Veterans of Foreign Wars had under consider-
ation the matter of recommending Congressional action te recognize the service of
those veterans, the California Joint Imnigration Committee obtained from Consul Gen-
eral Shuh Tomii of San Francisco information as to conditions under which Japan would
permit the abandonment of their Japanese citizenship. With that information before
them the State Attorney General of California and the District Naturalization Officer
offered suggestions as to how Congress might corfer the desired privilege without re-
pealing or endangering the exclusion provision of the l924 act, and inviting opening
of the gates to Asiatic immigration thereby. Congressman Lea was asked to take charge
of the matter and the bill prepared under his direotion was given approval of those
who had investigated the subject on behalf of California.
These facts were made plain to those who were inclined to oppose the measure be-
cause of a belief that Japan would see in such a concession an entering wedge encourag-
ing later demand for immigration quotn. The assuranoe was als given that the desire
of the California Joint Immigration Committoe to have gracious acknowledgement made of
a service to the nation in no way changed its unalterable opposition to any modifica-
tion of the exolusion provision of the l924 act. As a result of that explanation no
one appeared in opposition to the bill at the hearing before the House Immigration
Committee, April 23rd. Representatives Lea and Scott from California spoke in favor,
as did also the National Americanism Ofricers of the American Legion and the Veterans
of Foreign Wars, while a telegram from the Californie Joint Immigration Committee
anmounoed its approval of the bill as "effective and properly safeguarded". The
measure was recommended out without adverse vote and Representative Kramer of Califor-
nia, a member thereof, was delegated to make formal report to the House.

"500,000 ARMED JAPANESE!"
The California Joint Immigration Comnittee, speaking for its supporting
organizations, the state bodies of American Legion, Federation of Labor and Native
Sons of the Golden West, charecterizes as a fantastic dream a statement made a few
days ago before a Congressional Committee to the effect that "there are r00,000
armed Japanese in the United States, chiefly on the Pacific Coast and in Hawaii",
and deolares that the uncontradicted publication thereof is likely to disturb the
friendly relations and mutual appreciation now existing between Japanese and Cauca-
sians in California which has developed since settlement of the immigration question
in 1924. As an indication of that sentiment, the American Iegion and the Veterans
of Foreign Wars are taking steps to securo from Congress such action as will permit
without modifioation of the exclusion provision of the l924 law, the naturalization
of Japanese veterans who served with the Colors during the recent war.
In disproof of the statement as to "500,000 armed Japanese", the Joint
Committee calls attention to the fact that the entire Japanese population in the
United States, men, women and children, is only about 300,000, almost equally divided
between Hawaii and continental United States; that in Hawaii fully two-thirds of the
Japanese are American citizens by birth, and that over 50,000 thereof are still
attending public school; that two-thirds of the entire Japanese population of conti-
nental United States reside in California, where close contact has impressed Cali-
fornians with their law abiding character and the fine standards, mental, moral and
physical, of the second generation.
That there are in Culifornit, among the few alien leaders of the Japanese,
sufficient able bodied men to maintain armed units for ulterior purposes is most
improbable; and that such units could be maintained in secrecy is still more unlikely.
It is fairly certain that the Japanese-American citizens Ieague, in which most of
the grown Japanese of American birth seek to perfect themselves in American citizen-
ship, would discourage and expose, if it could nct prevent, a movement which would
inevitably wreck the future careers of its mcmbers.
It is true that some of the Japanese in this state are violating the alien
land law, but it is also true that they are doing it under arrangement with Cauca-
sian land owners who profit thereby and are equally guilty under the law. It is
probably true, as charged, that the Japanese fishing fleet of high powered boats
operating in the southern part of California, is responsible for the surreptitious
entry of Japanese into the state and otherwise constitutes a danger against which
the states of Oregon and Washington have taken safeguards. But these matters can
be correoted by law and by law enforcement, and they do not reflect upon the great
body of the Japanese population in California.
California Joint Immigration Committee
V. S. Moclatohy, Exec. Secy.
James K. Fisk, Chairman
#417
March 2, 1935.
