THE JAPANESE LANGUAGE SCHOOLS
Criticized as a Menace to American Citizenship
Separate Japanese language schools are maintained by the Japaneso in Hawaii and
California, and the young Japanese are compelled to attend regularly the sessions which
are held before or after public school hours. The reason given is that better contcct
oan be maintained thereby between the children and their alien parents who do not ren?
ily learn English. The schools are used, however, by the Japanese Government to devel.
cpe and maintain in the young Japanese, who claim and enjoy American citizenship, a
Cominating loyalty to Japan's God-descended Emperor. The text books prescribed are
those used in the sohools in Japan, the toachers are Japanese, usually Buddhists and
always Shintoists, and the exercises are suoh as are usual in Japan for the training
of good Japanese oitizens.
In 1919 the Hawaiian Legislature was compelled, on demand of the Japanese elec-
torate, to kill a bill which provided that teachers in such schools must have a work-
ing kmowledge of the English language and of American history and civics. In 1920 a
Survey Commission of the Federal Department of Edudation at Washington after lengthy
investigation stigmatized the Hawaiian Japanese language sohools as "un-Amcrican if
not anti-Amerioan" and recommended that they be abolished. Accordingly, in the fol-
lowing year the Legislature passed a more strenuous measure, the operation of which
was bitterly fought by the Japanesc; and the U. S. Supreme Court in 1927 deolared that
certain features of the law were unoonstitutional. Since then these Hawaiian schools
have been controlled entirely by Japanese influences.
Meanwhile, California, after study of translations of the text books used in
the Japanese separate sohools in the statc, pnssed in the early 20's a law giving the
State Department of Education control thoreof and providing that all teachers therein
must be Amerioan citizons. A new set of text books in consonance with Amerioan stan-
dards was prepared, and after approval by Mombusho--the Department of Education of
Japan--was printed and put in use. The law was later rendered inoperative by the de-
oision in the Hawaiian case, but it is understood that the text books prescribed under
that law are still in use in California.
There is general demand on the part of the Japanese in both California and Hawai:
for new text books more in consonance with world events and ideas of today. Such books
if introduoed will doubtless be prepared under direotion of Japan, and will conform to
her ideas and polioy, with the object of making the pupils advocates thereof. Charges
were made before the Hawaiian Commis sioners of Education, December 1l, l934, and before
the Territorial Legislature, March 27, 1935,--and not since denied--that Japan plans
to distribute among the Japanese language sohool pupils in Hawaii "eduoational mater-
ial conoerning the vital national defense policy of Japan" (C.J.I.C. Doc. #407).
The diffioulty of developing loyal American citizens, dependable in a crisis,
out of young Japanese trined under such auspices causes grave concern to those famil-
Iar with oonditions. In Hawaii today Japanese comprise more than 50% of the total
tmel registration, and about 90% of them attend the separate Japanese sohools. In
aliferaia these sohools have about 15,000 pupils, principally in the Los Angeles, San
isso and Saoramento districts.
In California the Nisei--the second generation Japanese born on American soil--
mg to the doubtful position as American citizens they must oocupy in public
if these conditions continue. They find too that the pupils, because of the
y the separate schools, find themselves handieapped in their publio
tes thas the kmowledge of the Japanese language obtained under the system
ase in later years; while the additional expense inwelved is con-
 emtereained  many that if the Japanese language were
h sokeels, as are other foreign languages, the young Japanese
 mowledge thereof, and the separate ssheols might be

BAGDAD LAND & LUMBER COMPANY
BAGDAD, FLORIDA
Sept. 16th, 1935.
Honorable Millard Caldwell,
Milton, Fla.
Dear Millard:--
Just a note to see if you can give me some
information about a matter that has worried me consider-
ably.
When an American citizen goes out to the
Naval Station to look around, he is told he cannot come in
as this is Government Property and we dont want to reveal
our Naval Secretes.
When an American citizen gets within 3 miles
of the shores of Japan with a kodak, though he is just a
tourist whom the Japanese have encouraged to visit Japan,
he is immediately arrested and thrown in jail until our
Government makes them turn him loose.
When a Japanese Naval Officer recently took
pictures of all our South Florida ports recently, he was
arrested by the local officers (who did not know any better),
and as soon as Washington found it out, they freed him with
due applogies.
When we have any new secret guns, or pow-
ders, or any new munitions of war to demonstrate for the
protection of our country, all Americans are forbidden
to be within a hundred miles, but all the Japanese Naval
and Army men are invited to attend the revealing of our
private secrets.
I know I am dumb; but what is the answer?
Sincerely yours,
E. C. WORK.

Washington
Sep 26 1935
My dear Mr. Caldwell:
In regard to your letter of September 18, 1935,
enclosing a letter from one of your constituents,
Mr. E. C. Work, I have to inform you that it is gratifying
to the Navy Department to find such a citizen of the United
States displaying such a deep interest in a matter of great
concern to the Navy and having direct bearing on the
security of the National Defense.
In reply to the question brought out in the second
paragraph of Mr. Work's letter, I have to inform you that
all naval stations as a general rule are open to visits by
United States citizens during specific visiting hours on
certain days of the week. The Navy Department feels certain
that any properly identified citizen could readily obtain
this authority and would be extended courteous treatment.
The Navy Department has no jurisdiction in regard
to the subject mentioned in the third paragraph so no attempt
will be made to formulate the proper reply.
The fourth paragraph of Mr. Work's letter shows
his amazement at the fact that foreigners are permitted
to photograph the topography of the United States. It is
regretted that there is no Federal Statute, whioh makes this
practice illegal.
Regarding the disclosure of secret and confidential
information to foreigners mentioned in the last paragraph
of Mr. Work's letter, I must state that he has been misin-
formed as the Navy Department zealously guards all infor-
mation which if disclosed to a foreign power would re-act
to be prejudicial to the National Defense.
Sincerely yours,
CLAUDE A. SWANSON.
Hon. Millard Caldwell,
House of Representatives.

Sir:
Inasmuoh as your Department has taken official
oognizance of my oase by issuing statements to the press,
I consider myself free to oall your attention to some
faots oonneoted therewith, which, if they are not known
to you by now, should be in your possession.
Most important is the conspiraoy indictment to whioh
I have not pleaded as yet. This indictment is apparently
a blanket one, and, so far as I can determine, copies
the offenses listed in the Revised Statute in question,
so that everything that a so-called "spy" oould or might
do is inoluded.
The interesting feature of this indictment is the faot
that the two Japanese Naval offioers mentioned are now in
Japan. I regret this exoeedingly. Of course, if they
desired to waive diplomatio immunity, and were present to
testify, I would be readily aoquitted.
However, there are four more members of the Japanese
Embassy staff - three in the Naval offioe - who could
perform this duty very readily. Three of them could
testify, for example, that, as far as I am conoerned, there
was no dealing in "confidential" or "seoret" doouments or
SEP 15 1936)
other matters at any time, and that my function was merely
FILED
that of"advisor," similar, for example, to that of the
present U.S. Naval Mission in Brazil. Numerous other
examples might be cited.
The question naturally arises, why these names were
omitted in preference to Commander Itimiya and Commander
Yamaki. These names are known to the Department of
Justioe and probably to the Navy Department. Is it possible

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.99)
2 state dept - farnsworth
that you do not know who they are ? I shall be pleased to
inform you myself at your request.
Or, as it is oertainly reasonable to suppose, are these
names kept from the so-called conspiraoy oharges because
of the fact that the persons represented are aotually in
Washington, and because the resulting publicity would oreate
a diplomatio furore, or worse ? Or, that they might very
readily clear me ?
Your Department knows, and I know, that this is an
acouratestatement of the situation, and about fits the oase.
The result may be very pleasing. no doubt, to all concerned
exoept myself. The Departm nt of Justioe, the District
Attorney, the Navy Department, the Japanese, nd, 1 presume,
your Department of st't ure sutisfiel.
Even the Hearst news eyndicate, which, by violation of
their pledge is responsible for the present contretemps,
should feel that one more betrayal of confidence on their
part has resulted in a great "patriotio" service, (not to
forget the benefits of a possible increase in ciroulution).
I am not unaware of the usual diplomatic procedure in
cases of this kind: However, I am unwilling, likewise, to
saorifioe myself for such agreeable artifices of the "horse-
and-buggy" era. nd I am unwilling to be a victim of the
anti-Japanese jingoism of the Hearst syndicate.
Suoh an attitude on my part may lead to a certain

unfortunate, as well as the broader concern your Department
would have in my oase, I asked your Department to oonfer

affairs, was acknowledged, but nothing resulted. My
second was apparently handled by Mr. Carr's assistant, and,
so far, is unproduotive of results.
I am perfeotly willing to cooperate in any way that
would serve best the interests of the United States. But,
as a matter involving my liberty, reputation, and the
inherent right of self-defense, guaranteed by law, I cannot
sit by in idleness when by so doing I am literally a pawn
in a situation, whioh, at times, resembles an opera bouffe.
I would appreaiote the courtesy of an expression of

pugust 15, 1936.
A CRITICISM OF "PACIFIC RELATIONS" - Hoffman
Hon. Vierling Kersey,
Superintendent of Public Instruction,
Sacramento, California.
Dear Sir:
In connection with the attempt to introduce Japanese propaganda into the
publio schools, first called to your attention in June, 1935, permit us to submit
herewith a statemens with exhibits concerning "Pacific Relations", by alter Gayley
Hoffman, a text book for use in the public schools, and already in use, it is stated,
in some of the Ct lifornia schocls. The author, as appears from the title page, is
now connected with the puclic schools of Los Angeles and was formerly Director of
Currioulum in the Chico High Sohool: It is understood that he outlined the course
on Pacific Relations now used in the Chico School.
Apparently that book, so far as concerns its treatment of the subject of
Japanese immigration, is intended, as wes the H waiian text book on Japan, to deceive
the student as to the facts, and as to the proper conclusions to be drawn therefrom;
and to make of him an innocent ally in securing abendonment of our national policy
for exclusion of all unassimilable Asiatic immigration. The book fits in, whether
intentionally or not, with the general plan to which your attention has been called
already.
That plan was exposed in protest to the Hawaiian Commissioners of Education
in December, 1934; to the Fuw iien Legislature in March, 1935; and to your office by
a statement and exhibits from this comnittee on June 21, 1935. It was charged there-
in--and has never been denied--that at a Departmental Conference of the Japanese
Government on the 21st of November, 1934, r. Setsuechi Aoki, General Secretary of
the International Cultural Relations Society, said, "For several years the school
heads of Hawaii and Americe, with the eid of many more influential educators and
missionaries, made a thorough survey of American tcxt books for the Japanese society.
The survey revealed that if proner propaganda is systematically carried out in the
schools the American children will become strong friends of Japan". Mr. Okada, head
of the Bureau of Cultural Relations, and former Consul Genoral of Hawaii, was quoted
as stating, "That all the necessary arrangements have been made with the department
of public instruction, also with the varicus religious and social leaders of Hawaii."
The Japan Times ("English Language Voice of Japan's Foreign Office") in its issue of
December 4, 1934, declared that "The Society of International Cultural Relations at
its meeting Mondey afternoon decidod on a plan to be followed for the compilation of
school text books to be used at high schools in the United States for diffusing cor-
reot information on Japan". It stated also that the collecting and provisional com-
pilation of material for the purpose would be done by a comittee in Japan, and then
referred to the representative of the Institute of Pacific Relations before submis-
sion to a joint Japanese-American committee for final use. (c.J.I.C. Docs.4l4 - #409)
It was charged that in furtherance of that plan the Institute of Pacific
Relations, with the aid of the Society for Cultural Relations of Japan and the Depart-
ment of Education of Hawaii, prepared a text book on Japan which was used in the
Honolulu publio sohools, notwithstanding protest, during l935; that, thru gross mis-
representation therein of facts concerning the Gentlemen's Agreement and its opera-
tion, and Japan's policy and activities during the present century, the authors sought

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.99)
the students that the United States should abandon its exclusion pclicy
the gates to Japanese immigration.
In that and in later statements and exhibits offered for your consideration
as shown that efforts were made to have the book endorsed for general use in
blio sohools of oontinental United States; that the exeoutives of the Institute of
Paoifio Relations announoed in February, l936, that that organization had arranged
to have courses in Pacifio Relations and Japanese Culture introduced into l52 publio
high sohools of California; that there had already been introduced into the publio
sohools of Los Angeles, for guidance of teachers in suoh proposed courses, a book
written by a diplomatic attache of Japan and manifestly intended to induce favorable
consideration of Japan's point of view; that the Society for Cultural Relations of
Japan had arranged to present to certain schools in Southern California books of ref-
erence "to correctly advise students" as to Japan's policies and acts; that on June
30. 1936, a year and a half after the first protest in regard thereto had been made,
the Amerioan Council of the Institute of Pacific Relations, thru its Secretary, made
ackmowledgoment that the criticism of its text book on Hawaii was correct, and an-
nounoed that the book had been withdrawn. from use and would be rewritten.
No one will question the advisability of having in the high schools courses
whioh will correctly inform the pupils as to our neighbors on the Pacific with which
relations are becoming always closer, and particularly as to their history and ac-
tivities during the present century, their aspirations and established policies.
But certainly every preoaution should be taken that, under the guise of information,
there is not contained, in text or reference book, propaganda in the interest of a
foreign nation designed to further its purpose by creating false impressions in the
minds of the students.
"Pacific Relations", by walter G. Hoffman, may or may not have been in-
spired by the influenoes and policy which produced the text book on Japan as used in
Hawaii; but so far as concerns the chapter devoted to Japanese immigration at page
361, it serves a similar purpose, i.e., to deceive the student as to facts and there-
by make of him a "strong friend of Japan". The purpose of the author, as declared
in the preface, is to so present facts with pictures of existing conditions and past
events that the student may appraise current events accurately. But, in the chapter
referred to, there is careful presentation of Japan's point of view, and as careful
omission of the facts which furnish complete answer thereto. The student who relies
upon this text book for accurate information as to the Japanese immigration question
cannot be blamed for believing that the United States is wrong in the position it
has taken.
In verification of the charge mado herein there are enclosed (1) copy of
the Chapter on "Emigration"; (2) reference to some of the important facts omitted by
the author but necessary to a knowledge of the subject; C.J.I.C. #455, ".hy Immigra-
tion Quota Should Not Be Granted To Japan", covering briefly the reasons which aotu-
ated Congress in passing the 1924 law, and in declining since, notwithstanding re-
peated requests, to take up reconsideration thereof.
In conclusion the California Joint Immigration Committee, representing the
State bodies of the American Legion, the State Federation of Labor and the Native
Sons of the Golden West, respeotfully urges that use or recognition in the public
sohools should be refused to any text book which misrepresents known facts concerning
matters of grave import to the United States; and that authors of such books, or
agenoies whioh are responsible for the production or introduotion thereof should not
have place or authority in conneotion with publio sohool curricula.
Respectfully,
California Joint Immigration Committee

Dear Mr. Field:
Your letter of June 3Oth contains the first intimation received that the execu-
tives of the American Council, I.P.R., rogard with anything but stern disapproval our
criticism of the text book on Japan sponsored by that organization and used in cer-
tain public schools of Hawaii in l935. Your frank acknovledgement of the justice of
that criticism merited prompt personal acknowledgement, if not answer, but since June
15th, while enjoying a prescribed rest here, I have not attended to office work.
Your letter concedes that the book does indeed, as charged, misrepresent or con-
ceal the faots as to provisions and operation of the Gentlemon's Agrcement and as to
Japan's policy and activities during the present century; also that we wer right in
questioning the procedure under which material for the book was gathered under aus-
pioes of the Society for International Cultural Relations of Japan; and you advise
that use of the book has been discontinued,and a new unit written with aid of American
authors.
You are kind enough to say that we did & service in pointing out those and simile
errors and that you are grateful therefor. You feel, hovever that this committee has
taken unnecessary steps in giving the subject wide publicity before assuring itsolf as
to whether the I.P.R. executives "are earnestly seeking accuracy". You feel too that
we "on several oocasions have engaged in a crusade against one or other of your pro-
jeots and by implication against the entire organization." In justice to this com-
mittee and with a frankness inspired by your own permit me to call your attention to
the faots, and to suggest that careful consideration will perhaps induce you to with-
draw that criticism.
The California Joint Immigration Committee is maintained by its supporting organ-
izations--California bodies of the American Legion, Federation of Labor, Native Sons--
for the purpose of defending and promoting effective operation of the law excluding
from permanent settlement aliens ineligible to American citizenship. That law is the
only effective barrier against peaceful penetration by the unassimilable races of Asia;
and the California Joint Committee is performing without compensation to its members a
valuable servico to state and nation. Like any other agency performing a similar ser-
vioe it must at times take steps or offer criticism which run counter to the views or
interosts of some individuals or groups.
Japan in recent years has sought, with aid of American friends, many of them act-
ing innocently, to undermine that law and secure entranco for her immigrants by a sys-
tem of propaganda designed to mislead public opinion. That program culminated in a
bold plan to introduce into our public school system, under the guise of study of
Paoifio Relations and Japanese culture coursestand text books which would convert Amer
ioan students to the Japanese point of view. The plan was frankly outlined in the Jap
Times in December, 1934, and in the utterances of Japanese officials, as publicly
oharged and not denied. The text book introduced under auspices of tho I.P.R. in
Hawaii offers a striking illustration of the plan and the methods followed.
The book and the plan were subjects of protest before the Commissioners of Edu-
cation of the Territory in December, 1934, and of petition to the Legislature in

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
The protest was suppressed and the petition was pigeonholed, tho cov-
ports of the news agenoies. Use of the book in Honolulu continued and the
or. a representative of the I.P.R., was sent to Columbia University at New
seoure endorsement for its use in the public schools of continental United
On June 21, 1935, the Joint Committee filed with the State Superintendent of
blio Instruotion of California a statement of the case, and copies thereof were sent
to the San Franoisoo headquarters of the I.P.R. and to the Superintendent of Public
Instruotion of Hawaii.
In July the representative of the I.P.R., still at Columbia University, defended
the book in a published interview, the press in Hawaii abused the Joint Committee for
calling attention to the facts, while the executives of the I.P.R. said nothing and,
so far as this committee was advised at the time, did nothing. Finally a statement of
the case was mailed to each of some 2o0 members of the American Council I.P.R., and
thru them presumably, the executives were induced to act.
You will recall informing a Sub-Section of the Commonwealth Club of California
in February, 1936, that the I.P.R. had arranged to introduce into 152 public schools
of California courses on Pacific Relations and Japanese Culture. You did not, of
course, know that Japan was improving that opportunity by securing recognition in Los
Angeles of a guide book for teachers in such courses prepared by an official agent of
Japan and recommending K.K.Kawakami, Japan's longtime propagandist in this country, as
reliable authority on history und policies of Japan; and that the Society for Inter-
national Cultural Relations was providing for some leading schools of Scuthern Cali-
fornia books of reference and Japanese instructors to properly inform the students.
As you know, the I.P.R., at its biennial sessions has been steadily urged by its
Japanese delegates, many of whom competontly represent the Society for Cultural Rela-
tions, to give consideration to such matters us "racial equality", "Universal right of
migration", and "international control of immigration". At the 1935 session at Benff
a resolution was offered, calling for appointment of a special committee to report on
"international control of immigrotion". Those matters do not appear in the published
proceedings, but the effects thereof can be seen in the attitude of many members of
the American Council who have no opportunity to hear the other side from sources well
informed thereon.
The facts above presented and the conneotion between them are not generally known.
but with the fairness of mind so evident in your letter, you will concede that anyone
kmowing them and concerned in protecting our public school system against foreign
propaganda designed to attack national policy and national law, would reach certain
conolusions and aot upon them. He would feel that an author who would, either delib-
erately or misled by others, so falsify or misrepresent available records as was done
in the Hawaiian text book, should not be permitted to take part in any way in prepara-
tion of text or reference books for use in public schools.
He would feel that an organization which, regardless of the high character of in-
dividuel members thereof, was responsible for preparation and introduction of the text
book referred to under the auspices and in the manner indicated by the facts, could
not, with propriety, be entrusted with the duty of providing courses on Pacific Rela-
tions for use in 152 public schools of California.
He would feel that an organization which sought even by extreme publicity to pre-
vent consummation of the plan so clearly indicated wes performing a public service
and should be supported therein.
It is fair to assume that you did not kmow, or did not realize, what certain fac-
tors within the I.P.R. organization were doing; and your letter justifies tho confi-
denoe with which this frank statement of the situation, as it must appear to others,
is presented for consideration.
Permit me to express personally confidence in your sincerity and admiration for
your courage and spirit.
Very sincerely,
#458
V. S. McClatohy, Exec. Secy.
California Joint Immigration Committee

Dear Mr. MoClatchy:
I have before me a considerable amount of correspondence which has comc to the off-
ice and to Mr. Carter's office from you during the last few months.
I should first of all like to assure you most carnestly thet we are always gratoful
for the correction of error in tentative or final drafts of our literature. You have
done us a service in pointing out certain mistakes in tho first draft of the high
sohool text book on Japan and vwe are gr teful to you for doing it. Unquestionebly the
references to the Gentlemen's Agreemont in the mimeogr phed Hawaiian study serial
were erroneous, and there wero also other doubtful interpretations. In looking back
over my file I am sorry to find that suitable acknowledgement was not made promptly
to you for pointing out these mistakes. I think that perhaps this was due to the
public nature of your statements, and to your seeming distrust of the good faith of
the Institute of Pacific Relations in its oducational activities. Meanwhile, as you
doubtless have been informed, because of our own dissatisfaction with the material
prepared for the Hawaiian school system, the text on Japan as well as the one on
China have been abandoned and new units written with the aid of American authorities.
You are quite right in questioning the procedure under hich further material for the
enlightenment of American youth was bcing collcctcd last year under the auspices of
the Society for International Cultural Relations in Japan. Tho idea proved unworkable
and the Society has given up the project. In its justification it should be added,
however, that the sponsorship which the Japenese Society came to assume in this pro-
jeot was not originally of its own desire, but resulted from the recognition by
American members of the I.P. ., of which I was one, of the Society's exceptional
facilities for helping to meet a lack which was felt by many American school men,
namely of descriptive teaching materiel about contemporary Japan which was both
authentio and in form suitable for juvenile reading. The American Council will now
endeavor to meet this need without Japanese cooperation from literary resources
available in this country.
Let me again emphasize the point that we always welcome suggestions as to how our
work may be improved. It is specifically for this reason that we often issue our
publications in a tertative draft to allow plenty of time for criticism before the
final manuscript is prepared. Insofar as you have taken thc trouble to perform ex-
eotly this taskfor us on a number of occasions, we are grateful. we are sorry, how-
ever, that you find it necessary to go fer beyond the procedure of pointing out to
us our mistakes and that on several occasions you have engaged in a crusade against
one or another of our projects and by implication against the cntire organization.
It seems to us quite unnecessary, in view of our eagerness to receive suggestions,
to carry the question to as wide a public as you can command before first assuring
yourself as to whether or not we are honestly seeking accuracy.
Sincerely yours,
Mr. V. S. MoClatchy, Exec. Secy.
California Joint Immigration Committee

85 Seoond Street
San Francisco, California

FACTS WHICH AUTHOR WALTER HOFFMAN OVERLOOKED
Em the aceompanying extract from "Pacifio Relations", a sohool text book by Walter
Wesfmam, a statement of faets in connection with the problem of Japanese immigration
shod with referenoe letters (a) to (h). Paragraphs below similarly marked indieate very
sefly the impertant facts whioh Mr. Hoffman overleoked. More information in concise form
be obtained from California Joint Immigration Committee Dom. #455, "Why Immigration Quota
ald Net be Granted to Japan".
(a) The two basie provisions of the Gentlemen's Agreement, as imposed by President
pedere Reosevelt and stated by him, called for such control ef passports by Japan that
1) Japamese laborers should not come to continental United States; (2) Japanese population
oentinontal United States should not inorease. If Japan failed to secure those results
 alternative set by President Roosevelt was an exolusion set for Japanese.
(b) "The fear of Japanese immigration continued until l924", because by Japan's de-
berate violation of the terms of the Agreement, many thousands of laborers entered and the
apanese population of continental United States multiplied.
(e) The provision excluding all aliens ineligible to American citizonship was inserted
m the lsze immigration ast as the only one of five plans proposed which would effectively
emedy the situation without disorimination against Japan or surrender of our national sov-
creignty or Congressional control of Immigration.
(d) Provisions had been made for exclusion not only of the Chinese but also of all
other imeligible Asiatics, ineluding the Japanese, the latter thru the Gentlemen's Agreement.
The plam for exolusion of Japanese was the only one that failed. As that was due to Japan's
vielation of its term there was no affront to her in abrogating the Agreement. Instead of
resertimg to the alternative fixed by President Roosevelt in suot case, Congress provided
the omly effeetive remedy which would at thstime save Japan's face.
(e) Japam's quota, it is true, would be only l85 under the terms of the present act,
bat admissiem weuld have to be coneeded also to all of the other ineligible races of Asia;
and slight medifisstiems ot the present plan, some already urged, would multiply by admis-
siems. The exelusiom law and its basic principle, the omly barrier against peaceful inva-
sien of Asiaties, weuld be automatieally destroyed, and could not be replaced.
(f) "Memy" my "feel that Japam's status in world affairs ontitles her to this consider-
asism", but Unele Sam believes that his status in world affairs entitles him to the same
pretestion agaimst imdgrant invasion as Japan ereets, under Imperial Ordinance #3s2, against
all Asia, inoluding Korea, and sueh as Japan defers to without pretest, when imposed against
her by Eaglish speaking nations other than the United States.
(g) The sitwation is indeed "unpleasant". Japan's notions and demands have made it
 It should imdeed "be reetified", but by withdrawal en Japan's part of a demand whieh is
mes omly fustified by the facts but is entirely at variance with what she herself exacts
ander similar eiroumstanees and what she aecepts without protest.

DANGERS CREATED BY JAPANTSE DUAL CITIZENSHIP
A serious problem exists, particularly in Hawaii and California, because most of the
Japanese born under the American flag and exercising the right of American citizenship
till eleot to retain Japanese citizenship with its obligations, altho they are now
free to expatriate. It is charged that many of these dual citizens would use their
Amerioan citizenship under direction or influence to further the purposes of Japan.
As late as the early 1920's, Japan maintained in California a state within a state
in whioh every Japanese, whether alien or native born American citizen, was under
orders of Japan, in peace and in war. He was forced to belong to a local association
subjeot to the Japanese Association of America and all under control of the Consul
General of Japan at San Francisco. (Senate Doc. #55, 1921, p. 63; also Japanese Immi-
gration and Colonization - V. S. MoClatchy, 1921, Secs. 196 to 218.)
Publioation of the facts and the passage in l924 of that provision of the immigra-
tion restriction act excluding as immigrants all aliens ineligible to American citizen-
ship induoed Japan to put into operation on December 1, 1924, a new nationality law.
Under that law Japanese born in the United States after the date named automatically
lost Japanese oitizenship unless within 14 days they were registered at the Japanese
Consulate. The law further provided that those so registered, as well as those born
here before December, l924, could renounce Japanese citizenship by declaration at the
Japanese Consulate after reaching their 2Oth year.
Under that system it was assumed that dual oitizenship would disappear if the Japan-
ese themselves really desired to renounce all obligations to Japan as the price for
Amerioan citizenship. The records prove, however, that various factors, including
the insistence of the first generation, the pull of heredity, the Japanese law of
family, the teachings of alien Buddhist instructors in the Japanese language schools,
and the enoouraged study of "Japanese culture", tend to nullify the purpose of the law.
In Hawaii, for instance, "the total number of American citizens of Japanese ancestry
who had relinquished Japanese citizenship was, as of June 30, 1934, 34,270, approxi-
mately 33% of those eligible for expatriation". Of that number 28,459 are Japanese
born sinoe Decomber 1, 1924, who lost Japanese citizenship automatically at birth
under the terms of the law; 5,8ll is the total number of the e who lost Japanese citi-
zenship by formal declaration at the Consulatc after reaching the age of 20 years.
(Letter of Gov. Joseph Poindexter to California Joint Immigration Committee, February
15, 1936.) In other words, two-thirds of the Hawaiian-born Japanese still retain their
Japanese oitizenship with all obligations thereof. That situation is attracting spe-
cial attention because the Territory is now asking for statehood, and the Japanese who
constitute 38% of the total population have already passed every other racial group
in the number of registered voters.
In California it must be assumed that conditions as to dual citizenship are somewhat
similar, although tho figures are not available because the Consulate General at San
Franeiseo no longer kmeps statistics of such matters, as was formerly done. (Letter
Shuh Tomii. Consul General of Japan at San Francisco, December 19, 1935.)
The following facts in connection with the California situation are of interest.
he Japanese Amerioan Citizens League, a powerful organization with approximately 50
waker our fMag, many if not most of whom, it would soem, still retain Japanese oit
monship. It even admits the "Kibei Shimin", Japanese born here and sent in early ohil
hood to dapen and there brought up to manhood and womanhood as Japanese oitisems. They
all intents and purposes when they return here, alien Japanose immigrants who
leges of American eitisenship. Japanese authorities place the total num-

and a friend of mine of some ten years' standing, called to
give me some details of the Farnsworth espionage case. Bull
stated that it was his firm conviction that Farnsworth had
been engaged in espionage for at least two years past and
cited in this instance the payment to him (Bull) of $9o0 cash
this payment was made Farnsworth had no obvious means of

Bull then told me that the Farnsworth case broke as
follows. Some months ago Farnsworth approached a local news-
paper syndicate and informed the editor that he was a Japanese
spy. The editor asked for proof and seemingly was sufficiently
8
assured of Farnsworth's status as to offer him $10,000 for

Confidential File

to J. Edgar Hoover, who in turn put men to tail Farnsworth.
Bull tells me that they have enough on Farnsworth now to
hang him "as high as a kite". In this connection he stated
that Farnsworth, who of recent months had been living in
Cinoinnati, used to be driven to town daily by his second
wife, where he would leave his car, enter another, and drive
off to an unknown destination.
Bull tells me that the Farnsworth family have engaged
a well known criminal lawyer, William Leahy, to defend Farns-
worth; that if the case comes to court, no matter if an insanity
plea is entered, names of high Japanese officials, whether
actually involved or not, will be mentioned, with possible
subsequent embarrassment.
Mr. Bull stated that there was no doubt in his mind, or
in the minds of the Farnsworth family, that Farnsworth himself
is crazy. On the other hand he has an extremely brilliant
mind and is absolutely unscrupulous. Bull feels that if the
case is brought to court it may result in an embarrassing
situation for us in our Japanese relations and that it would
be better to have Farnsworth privately examined and certified
for commitment to an insane asylum, where there would be time
and opportunity, quietly, to get his entire story and to sift
the whest from the chaff. Bull emphasized once again that he
had absolutely no doubt that Farnsworth had been engaged in
espionage aotivities on behalf of Japan.

=== Page 287 === (confidence 0.99)
SEP 4 1936
Washington Daily News
Japan Watches U. S.
Elections With View
to Trade Agreement
Attempts to Have Commission
Established Delayed, Tokio
Believes, by Presidential Race
By United Press
TOKYO—Japan wants better trade
relations with the United States and
intends to make an active effort in
that direction as soon as America's
presidential election is out of the
way.
Attempts of the Japanese govern-
ment to have a trade commission
established have been delayed, ac-
cording to the interpretation here,
by absorption of the United States
in its presidential race.
The trade figures as released here
show that last year Japan exported
to the United States goods worth
555,000,000 yen, while purchasing in
the United States goods valued at
800,000,000 yen.
PAPER'S COMMENTS
"Despite this favorable situation,"
says the newspaper Asam, "the United
States ignored its advantageous trade
with Japan and has been raising
tariff on cotton cloth, slide fasteners,
woolen gloves, assessed a consumption
tax on rape-seed oil and is now re-
ported to be considering a tariff in-
crease on porcelain and hosiery goods
from Japan.
"It is the objective of the Japanese
government to have the trade com-
mission established between the two
governments and discuss those goods
liable to be subjected to tariff raise.
The proposal was made to the Amer-
ican government, and when Commer-
cial Counsellor Inouye now at New
York returns to Japan, the Japanese
government authorities will hear
from him the views of the American
side in regard to the proposal, and
they will positively call upon the in-
terested quarters to assist in the
formation of the commission.
PERMANENT
"The forthcoming America-Japan
Trade Commiasion is designed to be
composed of the authorities of the
Treasury and State Departments on
the part of the United States and
26
the Japanese Embassy in Washing-
ten, Japanese Consul General, Com-
mercial Counsellor, etc., on the part
of Japan.

In a statement with exhibits filed with the State Superintendent of Public In-
struotion, Vierling Korsey, bv the California Joint Immigration Committee it is
charged that a recont text book on "Pacific Relations", by Walter G. Hoffman, so mis-
represents or conceals facts in connection with the subject of Japanese immigration
it is claimed, ansver conolusively the pleas put forth on behalf of Japan.
The statement declares that the chapter on "Emigration" in the Hoffman book,
in Japan's frankly announced plan for introduction of prop ganda into our public
schools.
That plan was exposed in protest against the use in Hawaiian public schools.
and the proposed use in California and other states, of a certain text book on Japan,
because of concealmont or misreprosentation of well known facts as made therein.
Those protests were made to the Hawaiian Dopartment of Education in Deccmber, 1934;
to the Hawaiian Legislature in March, 1935, and to the Superintendent of Public In-
struction of California on Juno 21, 1935.
It appeared thorefrom that at a Depurtmcntel Conferenoo of the Japanese Govorn-
ment heads at Tokyo, on November 21, 1934, it was reported by tho Socretary of the
International Cultural Relations Sooioty, that a survey, made in Hawaii and the United
States with aid of Educators and Missionarios, "revealed that, if propor propaganda
is systematically carried out in the schools, the American childron will become strong
friends of Japan". At the same meeting the hend of the Cultural Relations Bureau
stated that "All the necessary arrangements have been mude with the Public Instruotion
(Lepartment and also with th various roligious and social loaders in Hawaii". (a)
The Japan Times ("English Language Voice of Japan's Foreign Ofrioe") in its issue of
Docember 4, 1934, declared that "Thc Society of International Cultural Relations at
its meeting Monday afternoon decided on a plan to be followed for the compilation of
sohool text books to be usod at high schools in the United states for diffusing cor-
reot information on Japan". It stated also that the collecting and provisional com-
pilation of material for the purpose would be done by a committe in Japan, and then
referred to tho ropresontative of the Institute of Pacific Relations before submis-
sion to a joint Japanoso-American committec for final use. (b)
Under that plan the text book on Japan referred to was propered in Honolulu by
representatives of the Institute of Pacific Relations and the Hevaiian Departmont of
Education, with assistance of Prof. Nisemiohi Royama, of the Imperiel University of

Most Esteemed President:
I am attaching some clipnings taken from the 'Fresno
Bee" and the "Sacramento Bee" which are self-explanatory.
Article No. l-- proves the craftinese nd iet,
under-handed methods used in obtaining possession
of other nations, vithout too much outward fuss,
by the brain-trusts of Japan. located in their
own nation and in our beloved United States.
Article No. 2---proves that Christianity is taking a
stronger hold upon Japan than the "Buddhists
here in America is allowing for. Then why this
sudden "wide-awake" spread of Buddha propanganda?
CHT
Article No. 3---I do not believe in the sincerity of
this spread of "christian" converts into the
various churches, not as long as the "Temple
erected to worship the Lord Buddha" is so brazenly
heralded to all Japanese people. I think this is
their method of spying into American "private
thinks".
Article No. 4---This Temple should be abolished immediat-
ly away from American soil.  Why was this privilege
ever allowed to foster here in America, where
Christianity is most alive? It proves that we have
had diplomats, or leaders, who were not keenly alive
to the interests of our beloved Nation.
Article No. 5.---Also reveals plenty of their crafty
methods.

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
--2.
I also wish to add a few of my own "private thinks" on
this vital subject.
In Japanese places of business, here in Auburn and else
where are pictures of the "High Priestesses" of Japan. I am
of the opinion that these pictures have a Japanese message to
American Japanese embodied in them. Also there are other forns
of picture displays of "Japanese omen and Men in various poses
scattered thruout the United States.
Have these Japanese not
become American citi zens? Then vhy is this Japanese propaganda
of Japan allowed to foster here?
If these Japanesc were loyal
to their citizenship of America no such nictures would be allowd
to be displayed in the rublic eye. I think it is a spy system
of theirs, as well as a method of conveving a nublic message to
all Japanese.
I also believe that this "Buddha" advertising is also a
orafty method of conveying secret messages to the Jananese here in
America.
Too many Japanese are allowed to purchase land here in
America. No Buddiha Temples should be allowed to be built. A
special agency should take the names and addresses of all those
Japanese who are followers after "Buiaha" and sried unon by cur
secret agents. All public disnlays of Japanese pictures and
propaganda of any kind should be done away with and the names of
the offenders taken nd put on record. All their fingerprints
taken for future use.
This is not mere play on the part of the Japanese, but a
hidden motive is in back of all their exnressions of "Buddha"
loyalty.
This Nation was founded for Christian purposes and not for
the worship of idols and other forms of foreign worshipful leaders.
Respectfully submitted,
Alyce.Jonos

A few days ago Hiss Habel Stevens, whose card is
hereto attached, and who is connected with the Stevens Roalty Co.,
894.2021
at Mamaroneck, N. Y., called at this office stating that she
desired to impart some information concerning a Jupanese named
Toichiro Ts ge, for such attention, if uny, as wis considered advisable.

referred by one of the officials connected with that office to us.
It seems that iss Stevens in connection with the real
estate company referred to, rented a house to Toichiro Tsuge, at 32
Mount Pleasant Ave., Hemaroneck, . Y., and that she also was
instrumental in renting a house to Ars. Tsuge's sister and brother-in-law,
the latter's name being Takeo Suyemasa. She s tated that all pe sons named
are intelligent Jupanese and that irs. Tsuge claimed thet when the
Lindberghs were in Japan they stopped with her parents.

sail from there on Hovember 17th on the SS HIK . .TS for Japan.
Miss Stevens stated after giving this inforration, that
the real purpose of her visit wus to relate the fuct that a Pew days
ago in a discussion with rs. Suyenasa regarding the hostile relations
F
now existing between China and Japan, Miss Stevens expressed her own
belief that Japan was acting inproperly.
Miss Stevens said that she
did this with the purpose of arousing the anger of rs. Suyemasa and
apparently she was successful, inas uch a. she  lated thut irs. Suyemasa
justified Jupan's action und furthermore stated that within two years the

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: February 2, 1938.
SUBJECT:
Offer of service in connection with Investigations.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Arthur B. Kepler, 2547 North Union Street,
Kokomo, Indiana.
Mr. Ballantine.
COPIES TO:
894.20211/200
REOETVEP
FAH EASTLIN ALTALD
Til
FEB4~1938
lee
Deparimen
1-1408
Mr. Kepler stated he belonged to no organization but
undertook commissions directly from clients to make secret
investigations. He steted that he had contacts with Japa-
nese, could win their confidence, and could manage to enter
their employ if his services were desired in connection with
the investigation of Japanese. He said that some years ago
he met in this country a Japanese named Kasuda who, he under-
stood, was now connected with some Japanese railway and who
had suggested to him that the Japanese could use his services
for investigation work in America. He also said that he had
recently contacted two postmen who had suggested that he get
1n touch with the Japanese Embassy. Mr. Kepler said that
he was mot seeking employment as his present business yieide
C

=== Page 337 === (confidence 0.94)
-2-
him a modest return but he merely wished to offer his
services in case they were needed.
Mr. Ballantine thanked him for his offer, said that
he knew of no type of investigation in which the Department
might profitably enlist his services, but said that in case
anything should ever develop he would have Mr. Kepler's
offer in mind.

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: February 2, 1938.
SUBJECT:
Foreign Propaganda.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Alexander Resin.
Mr. Joseph Ballantine.
COPIES TO:
1-1408
894.20211
Mr. Resin stated that he had a research fellowship
from New York University and that he was making a study
of foreign propaganda in the United States. He desired
assistence in obteining information upon:
1. How propaganda enters the United States;
2. How it 1s distributed in the United States;
3. Information in regard to the authors and
printers of foreign propagenda.
He brought with him a copy of a document entitled
Sino-Japanese Cris1e of 1937 distributed by the Japanese
Chamber of Commerce of New York and asked whether the
Department had any information in regard to ite author-
r
ship or by whom it was printed. He stated that he
himself had made some contacts with Japanese connected
with the consular service at New York-but that he
had

-2
had elicited very little information from them. Mr. Resin
said that he would appreciate any leads that the Depart-
ment could give him which would help him in the prose-
cution of the investigation he 1s carrying on.
Mr. Ballantine seid he woula look into the matter
and suggested thet Mr. Resin call again in the course
of a few days when Mr. Ballantine would discuss the
subject with him further.

The FIRE
He is a Colonel on the staff
of the Governor of Tennessee.
Mr. Macfadden believes that the body should be athletic not only in
early life but throughout one's entire career . . . not for the pur-
pose of performing physical stunts, but to insure the ambition, enthusi-
asm, and energies so closely associated with the spirit of youth, which
are so valuable, regardless of what one's occupation may be, if one
desires a life of outstanding achievement.
HENEVER visiting a gymnasium or athletic feld
and the splendid specimens of physical manhood
are noted, or a ballet school in which we often find
physical femininity in all its magnificence, the unpleasant
deficiencies of the average individual stand out with
startling significance.
Nearly all our people through systematic training could
closely approximate these fine specimens of human per-
fection.
And the value of a splendidly vigorous body is not by
any means confined to its physical manifestations. It is
usually accompanied by vitality and energy which is the
direct source of the enthusiasm and ambition absolutely
essential to a worth-while career. Such possessions give
one the endurance, the determination, the fighting spirit
so essential to a life of real achievement.
Activity is the law of life. Inactivity, disuse of any
power, insures its gradual disappearance.
During my sixtieth year a course of physical training
was taken to demonstrate what could be accomplished at
that age. Since that time only that amount of exercise
has been used necessary to maintain the vigor and energy
essential to a very active business life.
I am now within the first part of my seventieth year,
and I am undergoing a system of training with a view of
determining how nearly the feats requiring strength,
endurance, and suppleness can be performed that were
recorded in my sixtieth year. These tests will continue
throughout this year. But the progress already made is
demonstrated very clearly by the reproductions of photo-
graphs which accompany this article.
Very briefly, some of the high points of my method of
developing and maintaining phyaical energies are here-
with presented.
Can a man be physically fit at seventy?
Muscular exercise is the first requirement, preferably
Here are some of the feats that Bernarr
in the form of recreation. It can be walking, running,
swimming, dancing, golf, tennis, baseball, or in fact any
gor and energy necessary to a healthy.
game that actively uses all the muscles of the body.
But if the fullest attainable strength is sought, special
attention must be given to spinal development. The spine
and even flying are included.
from the base of the skull to its lowest lumber vertebra.

Mv dear Mr. President:
Secretary to the President
In view of the menace to which our Nation is exrosed,
over a wide area in the Pacific Coastal regjons, it might be
timely for me to suggest, that a comprehensive survey of the
situation, insofar, as it afiects the welfare and safety of
the Nation be undertaken forthwith, without any undue delay.
Of mature years, and a keen student of Japan's un-
precedented strides in the van of progress during the last
few decades, and later in Southern California, having had
ample opportunity to study and scrutinize the situation at
first hand, I feel the urge and competency to make any and
many suggestions.
202
iithout doubt, the " ons of ipron," who enjoy much
latitude, afforded by our Democratic institutions on our
shores, have penetrated the Immigration barriers with much
sublety, and at will: they can be numbered by the legion
and in many instances, their resemblance to one another is
2
so remarkable, that they are comparable to a "bushel of
peas" and can only be segregated by means of finger-print-
ing or identificati on tag.

Japai. se fishermen, in their ocean-going and radio-equipne
"TLED

const nt contact at sea with Nipponese liners, bringing nes
aliens d other contraband to our shores.
1938

can, and cannot establish a residence on Chinese sofl, over
which she has nothing but a usurpers right, it might be
timely for the American Nation to afford the identical priv-
1leges to Japanese, that are extended to the mere handful
of United States citizens, resident in Nippon.
The presence of countless Japanese on our Western
continent, be they of alien or American birth, because a

Police Question
Two Jap Officers
Boston, March 21.—(AP)—Two
Japanese naval officers were de-
tained several hours Monday for
questioning by police after making
photographs in the vicinity of a
bridge over the Charles river. The
men, who identified themselves as
Lieutenant Commander
Mishimura and Engineering Of-
ficer S. Ito. explained that they
were pholographic enthusiasts and
that the pictures they made were
without military significance. Po-
lice accepted their explanalon and
aid immigration authorities had
found their passports in order. The
pair said they came to Cambridge
on business and were merely fol-
lowing their hobby of photography.

that the Attorney General submit this to the Department of State
as well for investigation. In explanation for this letter to
you will say thet we live here on cur farm 20 miles north of
Salina, Kansas, and have 2 Philcc all-vave world-vide recertion
battery receiving sets, one a 1: 30 no. 643 and also the new 1938
no. 643. With both: of ti ese sets witl. aerials up 4c feet it is
possible to reach all continents with nearly perfect recention
every twenty-four hours. In tris way I get direct, over station
JZJ Tokyo,Japan, 11.800 mag. per sec., 25.42 meters, every morning
from 7 to 8:30 AM CST here 8 to 9.30 E.ST. This Broadcast is
directed to the Malay Straits, Philippines, Indochina, and Australia.
It contains news in Japanese, French, Chinese, and English at
7.55 to 8.05 AM, CST S.55 to 9.05 AM, EST, and 10.55 to 11.05 PM
Japanese time 9 & ter hours difference in time from the two points.
The situation is simply tlis. I wish to call the Attorney Gener-

On January 25, 1938, Captain Allan 8. Farquhar,
United States Navy, Aoting Director of Navy Intelli-
gence, brought to the notice of Dr. Hornbeok propa-
ganda material obtained from the Japanese Consulate at
Chicago. "That office", he wrote, "is filled with boxes
of this propaganda which is currently being distributed
894.20211/06
by the Japanese Consul."
The material in question consisted of pamphlets
or regular issues of periodioals dealing with the Sino-
Japanese conflict, a list of which is as follows:
I. Published in Japan
A. By the Japan-Pacific Association, Tokyo:

chiefly translations of Japanese official

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.98)

II. Published in America
1. The Pamphleteer
2. What is the Fighting About? (Published by
the California Committee of Pacific Friend-
ship)
3. Problems of the Far East (Published by the
Committee of Japanese Residents in Chicago)
4. The Japanese American (Name and address of
publishers not given)
5. A pamphlet by the Society for the Promotion
of Paoific Peace.
Some of the foregoing material is not of an ob-
jeotionable character. The Tokyo Gazette contains refer-
ence material of real value, objectively presented, and
it is not believed that exception should be taken to
Japanese consuls making this publication available to
callers. The Osaka-Mainichi, while it of course pre-
sents only the Japanese side of the conflict, is an
ordinary commercial publication and the propaganda
aspect is incidental. The other material published in
Japan is pure propaganda published for the benefit of
English speaking people, and much of it is objectionable
because of its deliberately misleading oharaoter.
Some of the material published in the United States
1s

=== Page 379 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
is obviously paid Japanese propaganda; the rest is
probably also paid Japanese propaganda in a disguised
form. As suoh it is objeotionable and should be dif-
ferentiated from material which reflects the honest
opinions of publicists in this country.
Enclosure M, in the form of a handbill, is of a
blatantly propaganda character. It likens the situation
in China to a quarrel among neighbors and asks what the
reader would do in the same circumstances, justifies
Japan's actions, compares China's trade relations with
the United States with the greater Japanese-American
volume of trade, and quotes one of Boake Carter's columns
(in the New York Daily Mirror, October 21, 1937), the
olosing passage of which reads as follows:
"I'll give you a tip! The State Depart-
ment is run by Messrs. Norman Davis, Bili Bullitt,
Sumner Welles & Co. All of them have latohkeys
to Great Britain's Foreign Office. Great Britain
stands to be the big loser in the Oriental War -
not the United States."

MR. WELLES
Mr. Becretary:
The file hereunder relates to printed
materials of a propaganda character which
it is charged are being distributed by the
Japanese Consulate General in Chicago.
There is reason to believe that
materials of this character are being
distributed on a large scale by Japanese
official agencies in this country.
You need not trouble at this moment to
look through this file. I bring the matter
to your attention at this moment and in
connection with this case for the purpose
of suggesting that you give special thought
to the general question of the increasing
amount of propaganda which is being engaged
in in this country by foreign governments
for the purpose of influencing the American
public in matters of relations between the
United States and the foreign countries
which indulge in this practice.
Has not the time come, or is it not
rapialy approaching, when this Government
should give very serious consideration to
the question whether it is expedient and
desirable

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON POLITICAL RELATIONS
- 2 -
desirable that this type of thing be permitted
to go on unchecked, unregulated and even --
for commercial and other reasons -- encouraged
(by some of our own people)?9 So far has this
thing gone that at present certain firms
of consultants, business advisers, public
agents, etc., of American nationality, are
preparing and distributing, at the instance
of and with pay from official agencies of
foreign governnents, and without indication
of the ultimate source and background,
materials for the use of editorial writers,
speakers, lecturers, and even American offi-
cials.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
fis
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
March 24, 1938.
Reference the attached memorandum in regard to
material obtained by the Office of Naval Intelligence
from the Japanese Consulate in Chicago.
The propaganda character of the material is ob-
vious. The plain intent of the pamphlets and papers
894.20211/207
is to influence readers to view favorably Japanese
action and policy in China. Although much of the
material is objectionable in that it contains over-
statements, understatements, and misstatements cal-
culated to show Japan in a favorable light, it is not
of a character which from a strictly American point
of view might be definitely labeled as objectionable;
that is, it is not subversive and does not appear to
contain statements derogatory : the American Govern-
ment and officials and to American institutions, in-
terests, and people. (Exception is made in the case
of the folder quoting a press article by Boake Carter
which contains a remark regarding State Department
officers. However, although the remark is objectionable,
its inclusion in the quoted article seems to be, in so
F/WB
far as the purpose of the folder is concerned, acci-
π
dental and meaningless.)
MAY 3-1938
Part
FILED

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
Part of the material is published in Amerioa;
part in Japan. The American publishers would seem
to be within their rights in publishing the material
examined. With regard to the material published in
Japan, it would appear that the publishers are reput-
able Japanese organizations and that the character of
the material does not afford grounds for official ob-
jection -o its importation into this country.
It is not clear whether the Japanese Consul in
Chicago is actively distributing the material or
whether he is simply making it available to callers
at the Consulate. In the absence of information to
the contrary, it would not be advisable to assume that
he is doing more than giving the material to callers
at the Consulate.
In the light of the foregoing facts and views,
it is believed that the Department should not take
official cognizance of the matter. It is suggested,
however, that an officer of the Far Eastern Division
might appropriately bring the matter to the attention
of the Counselor of the Japanese Embassy when he next
calls, pointing out in a friendly manner the un-
desirability of Japanese officials acting as propaganda
agents in this country. Occasion might be taken, without
indioating

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.98)
-3
indicating the channel through whioh the Department
received the material under reference, to show the
Counselor some of the material including the folder
quoting a press article by Boake Carter. It might be
indicated to the Counselor that a number of Americans
have inquired of the Department in regard to the dis-
tribution of propaganda material by Japanese consuls,
and it might be suggested to him that this situation
is being brought to his attention in an informal way
inasmuch as it is felt that the Embassy would probably
wish to have Japanese consuls refrain from activities
which might become the subject of criticism.

American Society of Military Engineers,
Washington, D.C.
Gentlemen:
I am located in the Séerra Nevada Mts.
of California and see many actions on the part of
the Japanese that I do not personally agree to.
Only to-day I saw two machines of Japanese
photographing our Railroads, Highways and Bridges
that pass over the summit of these mountains.
The Japanese were not only photographing
these vital lines of transportation at critical points
but were also taking notes. I have seen this being done
openly by the Japanese upon several accasions during
the last two years.
It is true that we live in a free country
but the question arises in my mind if our cauntry should
be quite this fmee to a foreign power.
I at least feel that thes information should
be given to the proper military authorities that they
may act on the matter as their judgement directs.
Knowledge on their part of such action of a
foreign power can at least do no harm.
JUL1 2 1938
Weeks, M.E.M.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
NOV 1 5 1938
2.9.c.
Dear Mr. President;
Department of State
Referring to my letter early this month from Victoria, B. C. re-
garding a report that a Japanse aquadron had been sighted September 27-28, 250 miles
off Vancouver Island by a British tramp steamer, I took the report to the Staff of-
ficer of the Intelligence Department at Esquimalt, Victoria, who thanked me, and
stated that the report had not reached the department, but that they were glad to
have any such turned in at once, whether they proved true or false.
I finally ran the tale to earth at one of the local newspaper offices, where it
was pronounced without foundation; but on consideration, both my informant and I
agreed that it would be pronounced false by said publication in any event.
My informant has now, I believe, left for England, and if I can leann the name
of the vessel next month in Victoria, I will wire him to make connection with the
officers of the boat in question. The matter may be important for both countries
894.20211/218
and for yourself, for whom else have we suited to lead our country in a war of self-
preservation?
England shows us what can happen to a country with a leader of very ordinary abil-
ity,"suited for mayor of a small city in quiet times." Unless she is now willing to
become a vassal state, it looks as if war might break at any time, when Japan may en-
circle or attack Hongkong, possibly bringing in the American navy, and may consider
herself in the same position which she occupied on Sept. 27.
If Mr. Lindbergh is still an American citizen, it would seem that he should di-
vulge his reason for believing the German air force such as he is reported as rating
it. One can hardly avoid the suspicion that the "intelligence experts" who have stated
that Germany has looo0 planes ready to take off at an hour's notice, may have been
duped. Such a force would cover a full square mile, if ready for such quick take-off.
If they were housed at several air ports, were they all visited and were the planes
tested at random;- or were they dummies, with no machinery? A visitor would hardly
feel free to test that matter by disclosing scepticism.
Professor Frederick Schuman now publishes the statement tht the war scare of
last month was manufactured. My informant, who knows Mr. Chamverlain personally,
told me nearly a month ago that the whoaffair was staged.

THE PITTOBURON COURIER
(Weekly Wegro Wewspaper)
JAPANISE SCHOLAR OFFERS PREE TRANSPORTATION 'BACK-TO-AFRICA'
TEREE MILLION AMERICAN WEGROES ASKED TO TAKE PART IN
JAPANESE COLONIZATION SCHEME - BUT WHERE IS THE LAND COMING FROM ?
Free transportation from Los Angeles, Cal., to Africa on
Japamese shkps, 75 acres of land, a house, farm implements, animals
and crop seed, all free, with a ten-year cultivation ownership plan
under the sponsorship of the Japanese Government were offered last
Monday night in a meeting at Charles Street and Melrose Avenue to
the North Side members of Unit No. 3 of thePacific Movement of
the Bastern World by the organization's guiding figure in America-
Dr. Itake Coo, Chieago University graduate, son of a Mexican mother
and Japanese father.
Three million American Negroes are being invited by Dr. Coo
to avail themselves of the tremendous and daring Japanese Coloni-
sation project which is so near ready that applications, similar
to passports in substance, are available.
The learned mative of the Land of the Rising Sun painted a
vivid and happy word picture of the wholesale migration to Africa,
and ple ged the Japanese Government's wholehearted support, but
failed to state that Japan does not owm or control a square mile
on the Dark Continent to date. When asked by the Pittsburgh
Courier reporter what part of Africa was to be colonized by his

=== Page 437 === (confidence 0.98)
ese Colomisation Sehege
Po The Pittaburgh Courior - 2
country -- wcald be "somewhere about the Gold Coast."
Dr. Coo, returning to the City after a five-year absence,
was seathing in his denunciation of the Negro for failing to
get together under intelligent leadership, and urged that Negro
delegates be sent to Japan for inspiration and instruction.
Beonomic conditions of Negroes were roundly scored as were
seneral living eonditions and racial prejudice.
JAPANESE WATCH US
In a burst of oratory, the dynamic Oriental scholar warned
American colored peoples to stir themselves into intelligent
aetion before the Japanese Government be obliged to stir them
by force. Lothrop Stoddard's "Rising Tide of Color" was quoted
to prove that Japan was destined to organize all the dark races
of the world and lead them in a grand triumph over the whites.

894.20211/224
2-1939
PA/HV- Dr. Hornbeck
FEMr. Hamilton
Mr. Raymond Fosdick, the President of the Rockefeller
Foundation, and Mr. Belknap, the chief Counsel of the
Foundation, celled to see me this morning with an extra-
ordinary statement, of which cnly a brief resume will be
given here. Dr. Hornbeck and Mr. Hamilton were present
during a nart of the conversation and have most of the
details.
Mr. Fosdick stated that in November of last year
Colonel Yamaut1, the Military Attaché of the Japanese
Embassy in Washington, wrote to the Rockefeller Institute
no
whom he described as a language officer of the Japanese
or
Army. It was desired that Dr. Naito be given an opportunity
dential File
/WB
to study as an observer in the Bacteriological Section of
the Institute. The permission was refused although there
was a good deal of insistence on the part of the Japanese
Military Attaché and Dr. Naito.
Last

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
Last week Dr. Naito called at the Institute with a
letter of introduction from Colonel Yamaut1 in which
Dr. Naito 1s described as a bacteriologist and assistant
professor of the Army Medical College in Tokyo and in which
the statement 1s made that Dr. Naito had just completed
some studies at the Robert-Koch Institute in Berlin and
was now en route to Japan. The Military Attaché stated
that Dr. Naito was particularly intercsted in the work of
the Institute on yellow fever and desired to secure a vial
of the yellcw fever virus. After careful consideration
1t was decided by the Institute that the facilities should
not be made available to Dr. Naito and that the desired
tube of virus should not be given to him.
Several days after this definite refusal, an employee
of the Institute was annroached by an unknown man who
wished to make arrangements with him for another person
to see him on a matter which was described as being of
importance to him. On Sunday morning of this last week
according to arrangement this second unknown person got in
touch with the employee of the Institute and, as it was
raining, got into his car at the appointed spot. This
unknown man offered the employee various sums, finally
reaching $3,000, if he would secure for him a vial of the
yellow fever virus from the Institute. The employee
refused to have anything to do with the transaction.
Fa1ling

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
Failing to convinoe him, the unknown man took the ignition
key out of the car and then left, driving off rapidly in
his own car. The employee immediately informed his superiors
who have every confidence in him.
The employee had no knowledge whatever of the attempt
of Dr. Naito to secure this virus, et cetera, and it seemed
therefore significant to Mr. Fosdick that this attempt to
bribe one of their trusted employees should take place almost
1mmediately following the definite refusal to furnish Naito
with the virus.
As there is no yellow fever in Japan and there are no
yellow fever mosquitoesthere,the scientists of the Institute
were unable to determine why there should be this interest
on the part of the Japanese in the virus. There are yellow
fever mosquitoes in China and with this virus a few cases of
yellow fever could be induced and the result would be, the
scientists say, a tremendously rapid spread of the fever in
1ts most violent form all over China.
Mr. Fosdick said that a careful check had been made
of the supply of virus and they feel sure that none has
been missed. He said that adequate steps were being taken
to protect the supply of virus the Institute has here and
in its branches.
Mr. Fosdick stated that the circumatances were such
that he felt they should be brought to the attention of this
Department.

=== Page 451 === (confidence 0.97)
-4
Department. We expressed appreciation to him for their
action and the Secretary received them briefly. Dr. Hornbeok,
Mr. Hamilton and I considered the desirability of searching
Dr. Naito and his baggage on his proposed departure on a
Japanese steamer from San Francisco tomorrow but, after
careful consideration, it was decided that, as it was fairly
certain that he did not have any of the virus, 1t was likely
that the search would not disclose anything end that it would
merely put the Japanese on their guard. We were of the
opinion, however, that the circumstances indicated a
sinister background. The fact that this Dr. Naito had
just come from the Robert-Koch Institute in Berlin could
not be ignored and certain circumstances recited in the
accompanying documents furnished by the Rockefeller
Institute make this the more significant.
While the information furnished by the Rockefeller
Institute 1s significant and interesting, it does not seem
feasible to take any immediate steps in acting thereon.
G. S. Messersmith.

Statement of J. H. Bauer
On Sunday, February 26, 1939, Mr. Glasounoff arrived at my
apartment about 2:00 p.m. and reported the following: In the morning
when Glasounoff arrived at work in his car about 8:30, he wes signaled
to stop by a person unknown to him on the corner of York Avenue and 68th
Street just as he was turning into 6eth Street. The men wes ebout 25,
inconspicuous in appearence, of medium height, with no trace of foreign
accent. The men looked at his license number and asked if his name was
Glasounoff, and receiving an affirmetive reply, seid he knew someone who
would like to meet him and who had some matters to discuss with him which
would be very much to the latter's interest. Glasnouoff asked the per-
son's name and was told that he would learn that later when they met.
Glasounoff's curiosity was aroused considerably end he thourht that this
person referred to might have some interesting news rearding his family,
and therefore agreed to an appointment. Some time ago a person unexpectedly
called at the Rockefeller Institute and brought news from his sister in
France from whom he had not heard in several years. Accordingly Glasounoff
and the person who stopped his car agreed on meeting the unknown person
after Glasounoff had finished his work. The time was set at one o'clock,
and the place selected was on Exterior Street (Madame Curie Avenue) on the
East River where the Institute wall facing the river begins, or approxi-
mately the block between 67th and 68th.
After finishing his work, which consisted of feeding the enimals
and taking the temperature of the infected monkeys, at one o'clock
Glasounoff proceeded to the appointed place in his car by way of 7Oth Street.

Statement of . H. Dauer
On February 23 Dr. Ryoichi Naito, who said that he was an
Assistant Professor at the Army Medical College, Tokio, visited the
laboratory, bringing a letter of introduction from the Military Attache
of the Japanese Embassy in lashinston. The letter stated that e catle had
been received from the Superintendent of the Institute of Infectious
Diseases of the Tokio Imperial University asking Lr. Naito to secure
strains of yellow fever virus for that institute. When I inquired of Dr.
Naito which strains re wished to have, the reply was that he woulc like
to have the strain now used for vaccination and also a virulent, unmodified
Asibi strain. I telephoned to Dr. Sawyer and esked for instructions in
this matter and suggested that Lr. Sawyer see Dr. Naito personally, which
he agreed to do. I asked Dr. Naito to present the lctter of introduction
from the Japanese Embassy to Dr. Sawyer.
Later Dr. Sawyer telephoned to inform me that he had declined to
furnish the virus, basing his refusal cn the resolution passed by the Far
Eastern Bureau of the Health section of the League of Nations and also by
the Far Eastern Congress of Tropical wedicine in which the governments of
India, Neatherlands, Siam, and others agreed to prohivit indefinitely the
introduction of yellow fever virus for any purpose whatever into the siatic
countries. After visiting Dr. Sawyer Lr. Naito returned to the Institute
and asked a number of questions dealing mostly with yellow fever vaccine.
He particularly wanted to know where the vaccine used in Lrazil is prepared.
I told him that it was prepared in our laboratory in Rio de Janeiro, and
that that used in Colombie and Central America was shipped from this
laboratory.

=== Page 489 === (confidence 0.99)
February 28, 1939
Statement of Dr. C. M. MacLeod
Some time about the end of November, 1938, Dr. Rivers, the
Director of the Rockefeller Institute Hospital, on the staff of which I
serve, asked me to receive a visiting Japanese physicien, Dr. Ryoichi Naito,
and show him through the Hospital. Dr. Naito had an appointment to come
to the Hospital on November 28 or 29. He actually arrived four or five
days later, explaining that he had been ill at the time of his original
appointment.
I spent about two hours showing Dr. Naito around the Hospital
and I later entertained him at luncheon in the Institute dining room. I
showed Dr. Naito particularly matters relating to our pneumonia work which
is in my immediate field. The only phase of this work in which he seemed
to take special interest was the media room in which our pneumococcus broth
is made. I offered to give him the recipe for this broth which is used as
a culture medium for the growth of pneumococcus. He said he would be glad
to have this and I left instructions to have the recipe drawn up, but he
forgot all about it or did not ask for it as he was leaving and never
referred to it again. In the course of our conversation he said that he
had been studying at the Robert Koch Institute in Germany where he had
made some researches into the diagnosis of Weil's disease. This is a
disease which produces jaundice. Yellow fever is another disease which
produces jaundice. Dr. Naito was interested in the differential diagnosis
of the two diseases, Weil's disease and yellow fever. He discussed with me
a special form of technic for diagnosing Weil's disease by means of a
complement fixation reaction. Dr. Naito's English was rather uncertain.
Sometimes he seemed to understand what I said, other times not. He was

=== Page 490 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
not fluent in his own conversation and I was unable to form any distinct
impression as to his scientific competence or experience. His inquiries
into what we were doing in the Hospital were on the whole quite perfunctory.
He made no specific request to see the other branches of the Institute work
and did not ask to be admitted to the yellow fever research laboratories of
the Rockefeller Foundation which occupy a part of the Institute's premises.
Several days after he had been here I rectived from him a courteous letter
of thanks and appreciation for showing him through the Hospital.
6mhacLeon
