=== Page 494 === (confidence 0.99)
February 23, 1939
Dear Dr. Naito:
This is a brief note to confirm our conversation of this
forenoon.
I regret greatly that we are not in a position to supply
you with a virulent strain of yellow fever virus to take to Japan.
I understand from what you told me and from the letter of introduc-
tion which you brought from Colonel M. Yamanti of the Imperial
Japanese Embassy in Washington that the virus was requested in a
cablegram from the Superintendent of the Government Institute of In-
fectious Diseases in Tokyo. Although we have the highest respect
for the Institute and would be glad to send the virus there if it
seemed justifiable to transmit it to any part of the Far East, we
feel bound by our policies and the opinion which was expressed by
the Advisory Council of the Eastern Bureau of the League of
Nations Health Organization in l930 not to send a living virulent
virus to that part of the world.
At the same time, we would wish to be of any assistance
possible in settling any scientific question in our laboratory with
regard to the yellow fever virus on receiving the suggestions of
the Director of the Institute. I again express for myself and Dr.
Bauer our regret that we are unable to forward the specimen requested.
Sincerely yours,
Dr. Rydichi Naito,
(Signed)
Wilbur A. Sawyer
Nippon Club,
167 West 93rd Street,
New York, N. Y.

=== Page 495 === (confidence 0.99)
The Hospital of the Rockefeller Institute
For Medical Research
66th Street and York Avenue, New York
November 24, 1938
Dear Dr. Gasser:
After having finished reading your correspondence with
Colonel M. Yamauti of the Imperial Japanese Embassy in Washington,
I discussed with Dr. Avery the possibility of having Dr. Naito
spend several months in his laboratory. Dr. Avery and I both believe
that such an arrangement is entirely impossible. In the first place,
as you have already indicated, it is not the policy of the Institute
to take observers or volunteers for a short period of time. Secondly,
if it were the policy, it would be at this time impossible take Dr.
Naito anyway because of the absolute lack of space for further workers.
Dr. Avery indicated tnat he would be glad to have Dr. Naito
come to the hospital and his laboratory for a day's visit in order to
learn what is going on there. Dr. Colin MacLeod will be the man who
will spend most time with him and has suggested that next week would
be a suitable time, preferably Monday or Tuesday, November 28 or 29;
Wednesday and Thursday are out of the question because of previous
engagements.
Sincerely,
(Signed) Thomas M. Rivers
T. M. Rivers
Dr. H. S. Gasser,
Rockefeller Institute

=== Page 496 === (confidence 1.00)
November 23, 1938
Dear Colonel Yamauti:
I am in receipt of your letter of Nov. 18 and have kept the
subject of your request under consideration. In my previous letter I
feared that I failed to make clear the fact that voluntary workers are
admitted to the laboratories of the Institute only after formal in-
vitations from the Board of Scientific Directors, and that their
status then resembles very closely that of our regular staff. For
the reason stated in my previous letter, these invitations are seldom
extended for short periods.
In view of Dr. Naito's great desire to come to the Institute,
I have consulted the Director of the Hospital Department, Dr. T. M.
Rivers, and he in turn has talked with Dr. O. T. Avery about the matter.
Dr. Avery had already accepted more voluntary workers for this year
than he felt he could properly care for, and the opinion which he
expressed to Dr. Rivers was that it would be impossible to make any
additions to this group even on a long-time basis. I may add that
arrangements for the admission of these laboratory collaborators was
made a long time in advance.
We would be pleased to have Dr. Naito visit the Hospital
to see what is going on there, and Dr. Rivers will arrange to have
him taken about. Monday or Tuesday, Nov. 28 or 29, would be a suitable
time for him to come.
Yours very sincerely,
(Herbert S. Gasser),
Director.
Col. M. Yamauti,
Military Attache,
Imperial Japanese Embassy,
Washington, D. C.

Dear Dr. Rivers:
The enclosed correspondence with

=== Page 498 === (confidence 1.00)
November 10, 1938
Dear Sir:
Your letter of November 9 has been received and
I have noted with interest your request to have Dr. Ryoichi
Naito, a bacteriologist and a Language Officer of the
Japanese Army, study at the Rockefeller Institute for a
period of two months beginning on November 21.
I should like very much to assist you in this mat-
ter, but I fear that the organization of the Institute is not
such that it would prove of the greatest advantage to Dr.
Naito to spend his time here for a brief period. I should
explain that the Rockefeller Institute differs from the
American universities in that it has no provisions to enable
an outsider to obtain a general survey of a field of science
in the course of a few months. Our scientific workers are
regularly appointed for no less than a year to a particular
laboratory in the work of which they are especially interested
and in which they share under the direction of the head of
that laboratory.
For these reasons I am very sorry that we are not
in a position to accept Dr. Naito for the period mentioned
in your letter.
Yours sincerely,
(Herbert S. Gasser),
Director.
The Honorable,
Col. M. Yamauti,
Military Attaché
Imperial Japanese Embassy,
Washington, D. C.

=== Page 515 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
PA/H
Mr.Hornbeck:
COY
Mr. Ballantine and I have given further considera-
tion to the question whether it would be advisable for
the Department to make an approach to the Japanese Ambas-
sador on this matter. With a view to clarifying our
thought, we have prepared a draft of a possible memorandum
(attached) which might be handed to the Japanese Ambassador
as a record of statements made to him.
The conclusion of our further study is that no useful
purpose would be served by making such an approach to the
227
Japanese, either to the Ambassador here or through our
Embassy at Tokyo. In our opinion the letters written by
the Japanese Military Attaché to the Rockefeller Insti-
tute are perfectly courteous and frank, although lacking
in perfection in grammatical construction. The Japanese
have apparently accepted the position of the Rockefeller
Institute in declining to make available a specimen of
the virus. If an approach should be made to the Japanese,
Confidential File
such approach might induce the Japanese to contest the de-
cision of the Rockefeller Institute. And we believe that
WB
the Japanese could advance plausible grounds for contest-
ing that decision on grounds of scientific researoh and
need. Formosa is, according to our understanding, within
the

=== Page 516 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
the region where yellow fever carriers exist. Moreover,
neither Japan nor China is included in the area to which
the opinion of the Far Eastern Bureau of the League of
Nations Health Organization relates.
If such an approach to the Japanese authorities
would be likely to deter the Japanese from any purpose
they may have to obtain a specimen of the virus, the ap-
proach would probably be worthwhile. However, we very
much doubt whether the approach would have that result.
It would, in our opinion, be more likely merely to cause
the Japanese to resort to more underhanded and concealed
methods. One of the reasons why it was decided not to
have Dr. Naito searched was that the making of such a
search would put the Japanese on guard and be likely to
cause them merely to go about this matter in a less open
way. It seems to us that the same reasoning is applicable
to the question of an approach by this Government to the
Japanese Government.
We recommend:
(1) that no approach be made to the Japanese
Government;
(2) that no approaches be made to the British
or to the French Governments (the Rockefeller Institute
has already communicated with the appropriate scientific
institutions in Paris and in London);
(3) that

=== Page 517 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
(3) that upon receipt from the Labor Depart-
ment of its record with regard to Dr. Naito we consider
whether there is any information in that record which
might suggest an idea as to possible helpful action;
(4) that we inform in strict confidence senior
officers of M.I.D. and O.N.I. of the information con-
tained in this file and discuss with them the question
whether there are any preventive or precautionary steps
which it might be helpful to take.

=== Page 518 === (confidence 0.99)
March 8, 1939.
D R A F T
On November 9 and November 18, 1938, and on Febru-
ary 22, 1939, the Military Attaché of the Japanese Embassy,
Colonel Masafumi Yamauti, addressed letters to officers of
the Rockefeller Institute for Medical Research, New York
City, relatine to the desire of Dr. Ryoichi Naito, variously
described in the letters as a language officer of the
Japanese Army and as a bacteriologist and assistant
professor of the Japanese Army Medical School in Tokyo,
to study at the Rockefeller Institute and to obtain from
the Rockefeller Institute at the instance of the superin-
tendent of the Government Institute of Infectious Diseases
in Tokyo e strain of yellow fever virus to take to Japan.
While the Rockefeller Institute extended to Dr. Naito
the courtesy of conducting him through the hospital and
the laboratory, it was not practicable for the Institute
to arrange for Dr. Naito to study at the Institute. Nor
was the Institute in position to supply Dr. Naito with a
virulent strain of yellow fever virus to take to Japan.
The decision of the Rockefeller Institute was based on
the policies of the Institute and on the opinion expressed
in 1930 by the Advisory Council of the Eastern Bureau of

=== Page 519 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
the League of Nations Health Organization that the living
virus should not be introduced into the countries in the
Far East even for laboratory experiments on account of
the great danger of accidentally starting epidemics.
It is understood that there is no yellow fever in
Japan and that there are no yellow fever mosquitoes there;
that there are yellow fever mosquttoes in China south of
the Yangtze River; that should yellow fever be introduced
into China or other areas of tropical asia there woulc be
likely to occur a calamitous epidemic; and that scientific
opinion therefore dictates that every possible precaution
be taken against the yellow fever virus being introduced
into areas of the Far East which might be liable to infec-
tion with the virus.
In view of the foregoing, information is requested as
to the reasons why Dr. Naito and the superintendent of the
Government Institute of Infectious Diseases in Tokyo wished
to obtain a virulent strain of the yellow fever virus to be
taken to Japan.

hashi knew he 1s skilled in metallurgy and chemistry,

learning about poisonous gases, airplane materials,
molybdenum and certain other metals. Mr. Jenison repre-

hashi is an official of the Japanese Government.
Mr. Jenison is 62 years of age, apparently well versed
1n ohemistry and metallurgy, and thoroughly engrossed in
